From Local To Global Optimality in Concurrent Parity Games
Benjamin Bordais, Patricia Bouyer, Stéphane Le Roux

Abstract
We study two-player games on finite graphs. Turn-based games have many nice properties, but concurrent games are harder to tame: e.g. turn-based stochastic parity games have positional optimal strategies, whereas even basic concurrent reachability games may fail to have optimal strategies. We study concurrent stochastic parity games, and identify a local structural condition that, when satisfied at each state, guarantees existence of positional optimal strategies for both players.



1
Introduction

Two-player games played on finite graphs have been a helpful model in areas of computer science. In such games, states/vertices of the graph are colored; the actions of the players induce an infinite path in the graph, thus inducing an infinite sequence of colors. Who wins depends on the color sequence. These games can be turn-based, i.e. at each state a unique player chooses an outgoing edge leading to a probability distribution over successor states, or concurrent, i.e. at each state, the combination of one action per player determines the probability distribution over successor states. In such a stochastic setting, Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to maximize her probability to win, and Player
\mathsf{B}
to minimize the very same probability.

We study the above games in the case of parity objective: colors are
natural numbers, and a sequence is winning for Player
\mathsf{A}
iff the
maximal color seen infinitely often is even. This objective has been
well-studied in connection with model-checking. The turn-based version
of these games has nice properties involving deterministic positional
strategies, where ”positional” means that the played action depends
only on the current state: with only deterministic probability distributions over successor states, either of the
players has a winning such strategy
[
17
]
; with arbitrary probability distributions over successor states, both players have optimal such strategies

[
18
,
7
]
. Note that in
concurrent parity games, positional optimal strategies for distinct
starting states yield one positional strategy optimal for all states
uniformly. So we omit the word ”uniform” in this paper.

The above properties and others break in basic concurrent games but for safety games, as recorded in the table below, where safety and reachability are special cases of (co-)Büchi, and parity subsumes (co-)Büchi. Büchi games may not have optimal strategies but when they have, they have positional ones; co-Büchi games may not have optimal strategies, and they may have only infinite-memory ones; and the other way around for
\varepsilon
-optimal strategies, hence incomparable difficulty between Büchi and co-Büchi. This shows that concurrent parity is strictly harder than (co-)Büchi, since parity games may have only infinite-memory
(
\varepsilon
-)optimal strategies. (See also the column on subgame optimal strategies or Appendix
A
.)

opt strat?

\varepsilon
-opt

]

]

Table 1:
The summary of the situation in concurrent parity games in terms of existence and nature of (subgame) (almost-)optimal strategies.

Nevertheless, concurrent games’ poor behavior in general should not deter us from studying them, as many complex systems are inherently concurrent. See
[
12
]
for further arguments. Continuing a recent line of research
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
we seek local structural good behaviors that scale up to the whole game.

The following key property still holds in parity concurrent games, by determinacy of Blackwell games
[
13
]
: each state has a value
u\in[0,1]
, i.e. for all
\varepsilon>0
Player
\mathsf{A}
has an
\varepsilon
-optimal strategy for plays starting in the state, i.e. guaranteeing probability at least
u-\varepsilon
to win, regardless of Player
\mathsf{B}
’s strategy; and likewise Player
\mathsf{B}
can guarantee that Player
\mathsf{A}
wins with probability at most
u+\varepsilon
. Here we are interested in strategies that are optimal, i.e. that
realize exactly the value
u
, and that are positional. They do not
exist in general (see
[
3
, Figure 2]
), but our result below is a transfer from local to global positional optimality, and the terminology that it uses is explained afterwards.

Theorem 1
.

If at every state the induced game form is positionally optimizable, both players have positional optimal strategies in the game.

A game form is a map from pairs of actions to a probability distribution over successor states, see Figure
1
to the left, or
[
2
]
page 5 forcmore. So each state induces a game form. Given a game form
\mathcal{F}
, an
\mathcal{F}
-game has a unique non-trivial state which induces

\mathcal{F}
and the other player states are of two kinds: first colored kind, they loop
back to the non-trivial state; second uncolored kind, they have an explicit value in
[0,1]
, and the game stops there. Moreover Player
\mathsf{A}
prefers higher explicit values. See Figure
1
or Figure
6
for examples of such a construction. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
\mathcal{F}
is called
positionally optimizable up to
n
if both players have positional optimal strategies in all
\mathcal{F}
-games with at most
n+1
different colors. We say that
\mathcal{F}
is positionally optimizable if it positionally optimizable up to
n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. An overview of the proof of Theorem
1
is given in Subsection
6.1
, after having introduced the relevant definitions.

As a corollary of Theorem 1, we recover the known result that positional strategies are sufficient to play optimally in turn-based stochastic
games
[
18
,
7
]
.

The version of Theorem
1
that we show in this paper (i.e. Theorem
25
) is actually a little more precise. Indeed, for Theorem
1
to hold, it is actually sufficient that all the game forms appearing in the game are positionally optimizable up to
n
where
n
denotes the different between the highest and least color appearing in the game. After having proved Theorem
25
, we show that it is decidable if a game form is positionally optimizable (resp. positionally optimizable up to
n
), see Proposition
58
. Furthermore, we also show that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of game forms positionally optimizable up to
n
is strictly larger that the set of game forms up to
n+1
, see Theorem
59
.

Finally, note that the results presented in this paper appear in the PhD thesis of one of the authors, which will soon be available online.

2
Preliminaries and Game Forms

Consider a non-empty set
Q
. We denote by
Q^{*}
,
Q^{+}
and
Q^{\omega}
the set of finite sequences, non-empty finite sequences and infinite sequences of elements of
Q
respectively.

For all subsets
S\subseteq Q
, we let
\mathbbm{1}_{S}:Q\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be the indicator function of the set
S
defined by, for all
q\in Q
, we have
\mathbbm{1}_{S}(q)=1
if and only if
q\in S
. When
S=\{q\}
for any
q\in Q
, we write
\mathbbm{1}_{q}
for
\mathbbm{1}_{S}
.

A
discrete probability distribution
over a non-empty finite set
Q
is a function
\mu:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
such that
\sum_{x\in Q}\mu(x)=1
. The distribution
\mu
is
deterministic
if
\mu(q)=1
for some
q\in Q
. For all
S\subseteq Q
, we let
\mu[S]:=\sum_{q\in S}\mu(q)
. The set of distributions over the set
Q
is denoted
\mathcal{D}(Q)
.

For all
i\leq j\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket
the set of integers between
i
and
j
:
\llbracket i,j\rrbracket:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid i\leq k\leq j\}
. This set is typed in the sense that these are seen as integers and not reals numbers:
\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\cap[0,1]=\emptyset
1
1

1

This is done because, in parity games, the integers refer to the colors appearing in the game whereas the real numbers between in
[0,1]
refer to the values of the states in the game. These are disjoint.
. For all finite sets
S\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathsf{Even}(S)
(resp.
\mathsf{Odd}(S)
) be the smallest even (resp. odd) integer that is greater than or equal to all elements in
S
.

We recall the definition of game forms, which
informally are finite two-dimensional tables with variables, and of games in normal forms which are game forms whose outcomes are values between
0
and
1
.

Definition 2

(Game form and game in normal form)
.

Consider a non-empty set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
. A
game form
(
\mathsf{GF}
for short) on
\mathsf{O}
is a tuple

\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{O},\varrho\rangle

where
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp.
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}
) is the non-empty finite set of actions available to Player
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
) and
\varrho:\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}\times\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{O})
is a function that associates a distribution over the outcomes to each pair of actions. We denote by
\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
the set of all game forms on
\mathsf{O}
.

When the set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
is equal to
[0,1]
, we say that
\mathcal{F}
is a
game in normal form
. For a valuation
v:\mathsf{O}\rightarrow[0,1]
of the outcomes, the notation
\langle\mathcal{F},v\rangle
refers to the game in normal form
\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},[0,1],v\circ\varrho\rangle
induced from
\mathcal{F}
by
v
.

Finally, a Player-
\mathsf{A}
(resp. Player-
\mathsf{B}
) game
form
\mathcal{F}
is such that
|\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}|=1
(resp.
|\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}|=1
). The only Player-
\mathsf{B}
(resp. Player-
\mathsf{A}
) action is denoted

*
. Such a game form is denoted
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{A}:\varrho(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},*)\rangle
(resp.
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{B}:\varrho(*,\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})\rangle
). When
\mathcal{F}
is both a Player-
\mathsf{A}
and a Player-
\mathsf{B}
game form, it is called a
trivial
game form. Such a game form is denoted
\mathcal{F}=\{\varrho(*,*)\}
.

An example of a game form is depicted on the left of
Figure
1
where the actions available to Player
\mathsf{A}
are
the rows and the actions available to Player
\mathsf{B}
are the columns.
Strategies available to Player-
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
) are then the probability distributions over their respective sets of actions. In a game in normal form, Player
\mathsf{A}
tries to maximize the outcome, whereas Player
\mathsf{B}
tries to minimize
it.

Definition 3

(Outcome and value of a game in normal form)
.

Consider a game in normal form

\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},[0,1],\varrho\rangle
. The
set
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})
) corresponds to the
set of strategies available to Player
\mathsf{A}

(resp.
\mathsf{B}
), it is denoted
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
(resp.
\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{F})
). For a pair of strategies

(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{F})
, the outcome

\mathsf{out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})
in
\mathcal{F}
of the
strategies
(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})
is defined as:

\mathsf{out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}):=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}}\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}}\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(a)\cdot\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(b)\cdot\varrho(a,b)\in[0,1]
.

Consider a Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
.
The
value
of strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
, denoted

\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
is equal to:

\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}):=\inf_{\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{F})}\mathsf{out}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})
,
and dually for Player
\mathsf{B}
. When

\sup_{\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})}\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})=\inf_{\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{F})}\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})
, it defines the

value
of the game
\mathcal{F}
, denoted
\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}
. If

\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})=\mathsf{val}_{\mathcal{F}}
, the strategy

\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is said to be
optimal
for Player
\mathsf{A}
. This
is defined analogously for Player
\mathsf{B}
. Since the sets of actions is finite, Von Neumann’s minimax
theorem
[
16
]
ensures the existence of a value and
of optimal strategies for both players.

In the following, strategies in game forms will be called

\mathsf{GF}
-strategies in order not to confuse them with strategies in
concurrent games (on graphs).

3
Concurrent games, local interactions

3.1
Concurrent arenas and games

Definition 4

(Finite stochastic concurrent arena)
.

A finite
concurrent arena

\mathcal{C}
is a tuple

\langle Q,\mathsf{F}\rangle
where
Q
is a non-empty finite set of states and

\mathsf{F}:Q\rightarrow\mathsf{Form}(Q)
maps each state to its
induced game form, which describes the interaction of
the players at this state.

In the following, the arena
\mathcal{C}
will refer to the tuple
\langle Q,\mathsf{F}\rangle
, unless otherwise stated. Furthermore, for all
q\in Q
, we denote by
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{q}
and
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{q}
the sets of actions available to both players in the local interaction
\mathsf{F}(q)
, unless otherwise stated.
In this paper, we focus on (max) parity objectives: the goal of Player
\mathsf{A}
is that the maximum of the colors (i.e. label on states) visited infinitely often is even.

Definition 5

(Parity game)
.

Consider a finite set of states
Q
and a coloring function

\mathsf{col}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. This coloring function induces
the
parity objective

W(\mathsf{col})\subseteq Q^{\omega}
defined by

W(\mathsf{col}):=\{\rho\in Q^{\omega}\mid\max(\mathsf{col}(\rho)_{\infty})\text{ is even }\}
where

\mathsf{col}(\rho)_{\infty}:=\{k\in\mathbb{N}\mid\forall i\in\mathbb{N},\;\exists j\geq i,\;\mathsf{col}(\rho)_{j}=k\}\neq\emptyset
(since

Q
is finite) denotes the set of colors seen infinitely often in

\mathsf{col}(\rho)
.

A
parity game

\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
is a pair formed of a concurrent arena
\mathcal{C}
and a coloring function
\mathsf{col}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.

In such a game, strategies map the history of the game (i.e. the finite sequence of states visited so far)
to a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy in the game form corresponding to the current state of the game.

Definition 6

(Strategies)
.

Consider a concurrent arena
\mathcal{C}
. A
strategy
for Player

\mathsf{A}
is a function

\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}:\cup_{q\in Q}(Q^{*}\cdot q\rightarrow\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathsf{F}(q)))
. It is
positional
if for
all
q\in Q
, there is a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy

\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{q}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathsf{F}(q))
such that, for all

\pi=\rho\cdot q\in Q^{+}
:
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\pi)=\sigma_{A}^{q}
. In that
case, the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is said to be
defined
by

(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{q})_{q\in Q}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}
and

\mathsf{PS}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}
the set of all strategies and positional
strategies respectively in arena
\mathcal{C}
for Player
\mathsf{A}
. A strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is
deterministic
if, for all
\rho\in Q^{+}
,
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\rho)
is deterministic. The definitions are analogous for Player
\mathsf{B}
.

Unlike deterministic games with deterministic strategies, the
outcome of a game, given two strategies (one for each Player), is not a single play but
rather a distribution over plays. To formalize this, we first define
the probability to go from a state
q
to a state
q^{\prime}
given two

\mathsf{GF}
-strategies in
\mathsf{F}(q)
.

Definition 7

(Probability transition given two strategies)
.

Consider a concurrent arena
\mathcal{C}
, a state
q\in Q
and two strategies
(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathsf{F}(q))\times\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathsf{F}(q))
. Let
q^{\prime}\in Q
. The probability to go from
q
to
q^{\prime}
if the players plays, in q,
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
, denoted
\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}}{}(q,q^{\prime})
, is equal to:

\mathbb{P}^{\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}}{}(q,q^{\prime}):=\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathsf{F}(q),\mathbbm{1}_{q^{\prime}}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})

We now define the probability of occurrence of finite paths, and
consequently of any Borel set, given a strategy per player.

Definition 8

(Probability distribution given two strategies)
.

Consider a concurrent arena
\mathcal{C}
and two arbitrary strategies
(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}})\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}\times\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{B}}
.
We denote by
\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}:Q^{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Q)
the function giving the probability distribution over the next state of the arena given the sequence of states already seen. That is, for all finite path
\pi=\pi_{0}\ldots\pi_{n}\in Q^{+}
and
q\in Q
, we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}(\pi)[q]=\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\pi),\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}(\pi)}{}(\pi_{n},q)

The probability of a finite path
\pi=\pi_{0}\cdots\pi_{n}\in Q^{+}
from a state
q_{0}\in Q
with the pair of strategies
(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}})
is then equal to
\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{C},q_{0}}_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}(\pi):=\Pi_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}(\pi_{\leq i})[\pi_{i+1}]
if
\pi_{0}=q_{0}
and
0
otherwise. The probability of a cylinder set
\mathsf{Cyl}(\pi):=\{\pi\cdot\rho\mid\rho\in Q^{\omega}\}
is
\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{C},q_{0}}_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[\mathsf{Cyl}(\pi)]:=\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}(\pi)
for any finite path
\pi\in Q^{*}
. This induces the probability measure over Borel sets in the usual way. We denote by
\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{C},q_{0}}_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}
this probability measure. mapping each Borel set to a value in
[0,1]

The values of strategies and of the game follow.

Definition 9

(Value of strategies and of the game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
be a parity game and
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}
be a Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy. The vector
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}]:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
giving the value of the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is such that, for all
q_{0}\in Q
, we have
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q_{0}):=\inf_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{B}}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{C},q_{0}}_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[W(\mathsf{col})]
. The vector
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{A}]:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
giving the value for Player
\mathsf{A}
is such that, for all
q_{0}\in Q
, we have
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{A}](q_{0}):=\sup_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}}\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q_{0})
. The vector
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{B}]:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
giving the value of the game for Player
\mathsf{B}
is defined similarly by reversing
\inf
and
\sup
.

By Martin’s result on the determinacy of Blackwell
games
[
13
]
:

\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{A}]=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{B}]
, which defines the

value
of the game:

\chi_{\mathcal{G}}:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{A}]=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{B}]
. A
Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
such that

\chi_{\mathcal{G}}:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}]
is
optimal
(and
similarly for Player
\mathsf{B}
).

Note that optimal strategies may not exist in general; when they
exist they can be arbitrarily complex; see the table page
1
.

Finally, we extend our formalism by considering stopping states
with output value, i.e. states that, when visited, immediately
stop the game and induce a specific value in
[0,1]
. The fact that
the value of a stopping state
q
is set to be
u
is denoted

\mathsf{val}(q)\leftarrow u
. Note that such stopping states can be encoded in
our formalism (see
Appendix
C.1
). They will be
depicted as dashed states.

3.2
Markov chains and sufficient condition for optimality

In this subsection, we give a condition on positional strategies to be
optimal in a concurrent parity game. First, we need to introduce the
notions of Markov chain and bottom strongly connected component.

Definition 10

(Markov chain, BSCC)
.

A
Markov chain

\mathcal{M}
is a pair

\mathcal{M}=\langle Q,\mathbb{P}\rangle
where
Q
is a finite
set of states and
\mathbb{P}:Q\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Q)
. A

bottom strongly connected component
(BSCC for short)

H\subseteq Q
is a subset of states such that the underlying graph
of
H
is strongly connected (w.r.t. edges given by positive probability transitions from states to states) and
H
cannot be exited: for all
q\in H
and
q^{\prime}\in Q
,
\mathbb{P}(q)(q^{\prime})>0
implies
q^{\prime}\in H
.

We say that a subset of states
S\subseteq Q

occurs
in
H
if
S\cap H\neq\emptyset
. We say that a state
q\in Q

occurs
in
H
if
q\in H
.

Two positional strategies (one per player) in a concurrent arena
not only induce a probability measure on infinite sequences of states
(recall Definition
8
), but also a
Markov chain, whose graph is a subgraph of the arena.
(This is done formally in Appendix
C.2
.) If we only fix a strategy, we can consider the BSCCs that are
compatible with that strategy as follows.

Definition 11

(Induced Markov chains, BSCCs compatible with a strategy)
.

Consider a parity game
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
.
Let
\mathsf{s}
be a positional strategy for one of the players. For
every positional and deterministic strategy
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
for the other
player, we denote by

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}=\langle Q,\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}\rangle
the
Markov chain
induced
by
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
.

We let
H_{\mathsf{s}}

denote the set of BSCCs
compatible
with
\mathsf{s}
, i.e. the
BSCCs of some Markov chain
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{s},\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}

ranges over ther other player’s positional deterministic
strategies. A BSCC
H\in H_{\mathsf{s}}
is
even-colored
if

\max\mathsf{col}[H]
is even. Otherwise, it is
odd-colored
.

We now define several properties relating a positional strategy and a valuation of the states.

Definition 12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
be a game and
v:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
be a valuation over its states. Consider
a positional Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy

\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp. Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
).
The strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp.
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
):

•

dominates
the valuation
v
if for all
q\in Q
, it
holds that
v(q)\leq\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathsf{F}(q),v\rangle}(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(q))

(resp.
v(q)\geq\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathsf{F}(q),v\rangle}(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}(q))
);

•

parity dominates
the valuation
v
if it dominates
v

and all BSCCs
H
compatible with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp.
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
)
such that
\min v[H]>0
(resp.
\max v[H]<1
) are even-colored
(resp. odd-colored);

•

guarantees
the valuation
v
if, for all
q\in Q
, it
holds
v(q)\leq\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q)
(resp.
v(q)\geq\chi_{\mathcal{G}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}](q)
).

Parity dominating a valuation implies guaranteeing it (the theorem
below can be deduced from
[
5
, Lemma 34]
).

Theorem 13

(Proof
C.3
)
.

Consider a parity game
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
, a
Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{PS}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{A}}

and a valuation of the states
v:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
. If the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
dominates the valuation
v
, then for all BSCCs
H\in H_{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}}
, there is some
v_{H}\in[0,1]
such that
v[H]=\{v_{H}\}
.If in addition
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
parity dominates
v
, it also guarantees
v
.

4
Local Environment

The goal of this section is to define simple parity games with a
single non-trivial local interaction.
This will allow us to define exactly the game forms that should be
used in parity games if one requires positional optimal strategies for both players. We first define what a (parity) environment on a given set of outcomes is. We can then define the parity game induced by such an
environment (along with a game form).

Definition 14

(Parity environment)
.

Consider a non-empty finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
. An

environment

E
on
\mathsf{O}
is a tuple

E:=\langle c,e,p\rangle
where
c,e\in\mathbb{N}
with
c\leq e
and

p:\mathsf{O}\rightarrow\{q_{\mathsf{init}}\}\uplus K_{e}\uplus[0,1]
. We let
p_{[0,1]}:=p[\mathsf{O}]\cap[0,1]
. The
size
w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
)
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)
(resp.
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{B}}(E)
) of the environment
E
is equal to
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E):=\mathsf{Even}(e)-c
(resp.
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{B}}(E):=\mathsf{Odd}(e)-c
). We denote by

\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
the set of all environments on the set
of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
.

Remark 15
.

A quick note on the size of an environment. From Player
\mathsf{A}
’s perspective,
the size of an environment
E:=\langle c,e,p\rangle
, assuming that
c=0
, is equal to: 0 if
e=0
, 2 if
e=1
or
e=2
, etc. Informally, when
c=0
, if
e=0
this corresponds to safety game, if
e=1
this corresponds to a co-Büchi game, etc. This will become more apparent with Definition
16
below.

We can then define the simple parity game corresponding to a parity environment.

Definition 16

(Parity game induced by an environment)
.

Consider a non-empty finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, a
game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
and an environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
. Let
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. The local arena
\mathcal{C}_{Y}=\langle Q,\mathsf{F}\rangle
and the coloring function
\mathsf{col}_{Y}
induced by
Y
is such that:

•

Q:=\{q_{\mathsf{init}}\}\cup K_{e}\cup p_{[0,1]}
,
Q_{\mathsf{s}}:=p_{[0,1]}
, and for all
u\in p_{[0,1]}
, we set the value of the stopping state
u
to be
u
itself:
\mathsf{val}(u)\leftarrow u
;

•

\mathsf{F}(q_{\mathsf{init}}):=\mathcal{F}^{p}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},Q,\varrho_{p}\rangle
where, for all
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}})
,
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})
, and
q\in Q
, we have
\varrho_{p}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})(q):=\varrho(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})[p^{-1}[q]]
;

•

for all
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, the set
k_{i}
is trivial, its only outcome is the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
;

•

\mathsf{col}_{Y}(q_{\mathsf{init}}):=c
and for all
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we have
\mathsf{col}_{Y}(k_{i}):=i
.

For all
u\in[0,1]
, we denote by
v_{Y}^{u}:Q\rightarrow[0,1]
the valuation such that:
v_{Y}^{u}(q_{\mathsf{init}})=v_{Y}^{u}(k_{i}):=u
for all
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and
v_{Y}^{u}(x):=x
for all
x\in p_{[0,1]}
. Furthermore, for all Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategies
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
, we denote by
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{Y}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy defined by
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
in the arena
\mathcal{C}_{Y}
.

The game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
is then equal to
\mathcal{G}_{Y}:=\langle\mathcal{C}_{Y},\mathsf{col}_{Y}\rangle
.

Example 17
.

Definition
16
above is illustrated in Figure
1
. Note that the colors of the non-stopping states are depicted in red next to the states.

As in the previous chapter, we can define the notion of Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy being optimal in a parity environment.

Definition 18

(Optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategies)
.

Consider a non-empty finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, a
game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
, an environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
, and let
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. A Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
is said to be
optimal
w.r.t.
Y
if the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{Y}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
is optimal in
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
. The definition is analogous for Player
\mathsf{B}
.

Given a finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, we can now define the game forms on
\mathsf{O}
ensuring the existence of optimal strategies w.r.t. all environments.

Definition 19

(Game forms with optimal strategies)
.

Consider a non-empty finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, a game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
and some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider a Player
\mathsf{C}\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\}
. The game form
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
positionally maximizable up to

n
w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{C}
if, for each environment
E\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
with
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{C}}(E)\leq n
, there is an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy for Player
\mathsf{C}
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F},E)
.

When this holds for both Players,
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
positionally optimizable up to
n
. The corresponding set of game forms is denoted
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
. If this holds for
all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{F}
is simply said to be

positionally optimizable
, and the corresponding set of game forms is denoted
\mathsf{ParO}
.

Remark 20
.

First, note that all game forms are positionally optimizable up to 0. This is because environement of size 0 induce safety games. However, there are some game forms that are not positionally maximizable w.r.t. any player up to 1.

Furthermore, by definition, from a game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
that is not positionally optimizable up to
some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an environment
E\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
such that either of the players has no
positional optimal strategy in the simple parity game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
where the difference between
\mathsf{col}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
and the maximum of the
colors appearing in
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
is at most
n
.

In the game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
depicted on the right of Figure
1
, Player
\mathsf{A}
has positional optimal strategies: it suffices to play both rows with positive probability. (This is similar for Player
\mathsf{B}
.) As a side remark, the game form in the left of Figure
1
is positionally optimizable.

In Lemma
21
below, we formulate more explicitly (using the notion of parity domination from
Definition
12
) what optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategies are.

Lemma 21

(Proof
C.4
)
.

Consider a non-empty finite set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, a game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
, an environment

E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
and

Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. A Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
is
optimal
w.r.t.
Y
if and only if, letting

u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
, either (i)
u=0
, or (ii)
the positional Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy
\mathsf{s}^{Y}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
parity
dominates the valuation
v_{Y}^{u}
.

Furthermore (ii) is equivalent to:

•

(1) the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional
strategy
\mathsf{s}^{Y}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
dominates the valuation
v_{Y}^{u}
, i.e.
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is optimal in the game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{F},v^{u}_{Y}\circ p\rangle
; and

•

(2) for all
b\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}
, if
\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[0,1]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)=0

(i.e. the probability under
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
b
to reach a stopping state is null), then
\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)\cup\{c\})
is even where
\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b):=\{i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket\mid\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[k_{i}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0\}
is the set
of colors that can be seen with positive probability under

\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
b
.

This is symmetrical for Player
\mathsf{B}
.

Remark 22
.

Informally, this proposition states that for a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
to be optimal in a simple game

\mathcal{G}_{Y}
with positive value, it must be the case that for every
Player-
\mathsf{B}
action
b
: either there is a positive probability
(w.r.t.
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
b
) to exit
q_{\mathsf{init}}
and the expected value of the stopping states visited is at
least
u
; or the game loops on
q_{\mathsf{init}}
with
probability
1
, and the maximum of the colors that can be seen with positive probability (w.r.t.
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
b
) is even. In particular, if

c\leq\max\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)
or if
c
is
odd, then
\max\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)
is even.

5
The main theorem

\mathcal{F}:=\begin{bmatrix}y&x\\
x&y\end{bmatrix}

\bullet

E:=\langle 2,4,p\rangle
;

\bullet

p(x):=1/2
;

\bullet

p(y):=k_{3}
.

2
q_{\mathsf{init}}
,
\begin{bmatrix}k_{3}&1/2\\
1/2&k_{3}\end{bmatrix}
0
k_{0}
1
k_{1}
2
k_{2}
3
k_{3}
4
k_{4}
1/2

Figure 1:
On the left, a game form on the set of outcomes

\mathsf{O}:=\{x,y\}
, in the middle the description
of an environment on
\mathsf{O}
and on the right the parity game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
obtained from what is depicted on the left. The dashed state is a stopping of value
1/2
.

The goal of this section is to formally state the main theorem of this paper,
Theorem
25
below. Informally, this theorem consists in extracting, for every state of a game and for each Player, a local
environment which will summarize the context of the state to the
Player, and tell her how to play optimally (and positionally).

Before stating this theorem, let us recall and define below some useful notations, in particular we recall the notation for value slices.

Definition 23

(Value slice)
.

Consider a parity game
\mathcal{G}
. For all subsets of states
S\subseteq Q
, we denote by
V_{S}:=\{u\in[0,1]\mid\exists q\in S,\;\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)=u\}
the finite set of values of states in
S
. Furthermore, for all
u\in V_{Q}
, we let
Q_{u}:=\{q\in Q\mid\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)=u\}
be the set of states whose value is
u
: it is the
u
-slice of
\mathcal{G}
. Finally, for all
u\in V_{Q}
, we let
e_{u}:=\mathsf{Even}(\mathsf{col}[Q_{u}])
and
o_{u}:=\mathsf{Odd}(\mathsf{col}[Q_{u}])
.

We also introduce the notion of positional strategies generated by an environment function: this is a strategy that, at each state, plays a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy that is optimal in the corresponding environment.

Definition 24

(Positional strategy generated by an environment function)
.

For all environment functions

\mathsf{Ev}:Q\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
, a
Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is
generated
by

\mathsf{Ev}
if for all
q\in Q
, the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)

is optimal w.r.t.

(\mathsf{F}(q),\mathsf{Ev}(q))
.

This is similar for Player
\mathsf{B}
.

We can now state the main result of this chapter: Theorem
25
.

Theorem 25
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
be a parity game. Assume that for all
q\in Q
, letting
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)\in[0,1]
, the game form
\mathsf{F}(q)
is:

•

positionally
maximizable up to
e_{u}-\mathsf{col}(q)
w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{A}
; and

•

positionally maximizable up to
o_{u}-\mathsf{col}(q)
w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{B}
.

Then, there is a function
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}:Q\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
(resp.
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{B}}
) such that all Player-
\mathsf{A}

(resp. Player-
\mathsf{B}
) positional strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp.
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
) generated by
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}
(resp.
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{B}}
) are optimal in
\mathcal{G}
; and such strategies exist.

Remark 26
.

Given some
u\in V_{Q}
, one can realize that the requirement at states
q,q^{\prime}\in Q_{u}
changes depending on the color of
q
and
q^{\prime}
. More specifically, if
\mathsf{col}(q)<\mathsf{col}(q^{\prime})
, then the requirement at state
q
is (a priori) stronger than the requirement at state
q^{\prime}
since the game form
\mathsf{F}(q)
should behave well for environments of larger size than the game form
\mathsf{F}(q^{\prime})
.

The remainder of this section and the entirety of the next is devoted to the explanation of the construction of the environment function
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}
(the
construction being similar for Player
\mathsf{B}
), hence we are taking the
point of view of Player
\mathsf{A}
. First, let us argue that we can restrict ourselves to a specific
u
-slice
Q_{u}
for some
u\in V_{Q}
. Such a restriction is properly defined (using stopping states) in Definition
27
below.

Definition 27

(Game restricted to a
u
-slice)
.

For all
u\in V_{Q}
, we let
\mathcal{G}^{u}
be the concurrent game

\mathcal{G}
where all states outside
Q_{u}
are made stopping states:
for every
q\in Q\setminus Q_{u}
, we set

\mathsf{val}(q)\leftarrow\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)
.
The states, game forms and coloring function on
Q_{u}
are left unchanged.

Interestingly, a Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy optimal in
\mathcal{G}
can be obtained by merging appropriate positional strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u}
in the games
\mathcal{G}^{u}
for all
u\in V_{Q}\setminus\{0\}
, which is actually a straightforward consequence of Theorem
13
.

Lemma 28

(Proof
C.5
)
.

Consider a parity game
\mathcal{G}
. For all
u\in V_{Q}\setminus\{0\}
, consider a positional Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u}
that parity dominates the valuation
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}
in
\mathcal{G}^{u}
. Then, the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
such that
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(q):=\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u}(q)
for all
u\in V_{Q}\setminus\{0\}
and
q\in Q_{u}
guarantees the valuation
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}
in
\mathcal{G}
(i.e. it is optimal).

6
The proof

In Appendix
D
, we give a quick overview of the technical properties and lemmas that we show in the appendix and that we will use to prove the lemmas (we will state later in this chapter) leading to the proof of Theorem
25
.

In this section, we fix a parity game
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},\mathsf{col}\rangle
. In particular, the set of states
Q
is fixed: r ecall that
Q
is also the set of outcomes of all game forms occurring in
\mathcal{G}
. Also, Lemma
28
above justifies that we focus on a given
u
-slice
Q_{u}
for some positive
u\in(0,1]
. We also let
e:=e_{u}
,
o:=o_{u}
and
K:=\{k_{i}\mid i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket\}
and for all
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we let
K^{n}:=\{k_{i}^{n}\mid i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket\}
.

6.1
Big picture of the proof

In order to give an idea of the steps that we take to prove Theorem
25
, let us first consider the very simple case of finite turn-based deterministic reachability games. In this setting, computing the area
L_{\mathsf{A}}
from which Player
\mathsf{A}
has a winning strategy can be done inductively. That is, initially we set
L_{\mathsf{A}}:=T
where
T
denotes the target that Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to reach. Then, the inductive step is handled with a (deterministic) attractor: we add to
L_{\mathsf{A}}
any Player-
\mathsf{A}
state with a successor in
L_{\mathsf{A}}
and any Player-
\mathsf{B}
state with all successors in
L_{\mathsf{A}}
. After finitely many steps, there is no more state to add in
L_{\mathsf{A}}
: this exactly corresponds to the states from which Player
\mathsf{A}
has a winning strategy.

Computing a single attractor is not merely enough to take into account the intricate behavior of parity objectives, which is what Theorem
25
deals with. Therefore, we are going to iteratively compute several layers of (virtual) colors, with a local update to change the (virtual) color (and therefore the layer it belongs to) of a state. This local update can be seen as an attractor except in a concurrent stochastic setting. Hence, when we update the (virtual) color of a state, we take into account the
concurrent interaction of the players at each state along with the probability to see stopping states or states with different (virtual) colors.
We define this local update in Subsection
6.3
. Let us describe below the steps that we take to capture the behavior of the parity objective.

We compute layers of successive probabilistic attractors with leaks towards the stopping states. Although we compute a strategy, e.g., for Player
\mathsf{A}
, we alternate players to build layers, then move the last non-empty layer into the closest layer with same parity, then backtrack the attractor computation from this layer downwards, and start over again the full attractor computation on the new layer structure. In a more concrete way, let us assume below that the highest color in the
u
-slice is
6
. We proceed as follows:

q_{3}
3
q_{2}
2
q_{4}
4
q_{1}
1

Figure 2:
A (deterministic turn-based) game.

1.

Add the states colored with
6
to layer
L_{6}
.

2.

Recursively add to
L_{6}
the states where Player
\mathsf{A}
can guarantee that with positive probability (pp) either a leak towards stopping states occurs now and its expected explicit value is
u
or more (
\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
), or with pp the next state is in
L_{6}
, i.e. with pp color
6
or
\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
will occur.

3.

Add the remaining states colored with
5
to layer
L_{5}
.

4.

Recursively add to
L_{5}
the states where Player
\mathsf{B}

can guarantee that either
\mathsf{Leak}_{<u}
occurs now with
pp, or the next state is surely not in
L_{6}
and with pp in

L_{5}
; i.e. if
\mathsf{Leak}_{<u}
occurs with probability
0
, then color

6
will not occur but color
5
will eventually occur with pp.

5.

Add the remaining states colored with
4
to layer
L_{4}
.

6.

Recursively add to
L_{4}
the states where Player
\mathsf{A}
can guarantee that either
\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
will occur with pp, or the maximal layer index of the next states seen with pp is
4
or
6
, i.e. even and at least
4
.

7.

And so on, from color
3
to
0
. The layers so far only give information about what can happen at finite horizon.

8.

For instance, from
L_{2}
, Player
\mathsf{A}
can guarantee that either
\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
will occur with pp, or the maximal color that will be seen with pp is in
\{2,4,6\}
. Now, if e.g.
L_{0}=L_{1}=\emptyset
, we merge
L_{2}
into
L_{4}
. This is, arguably, the most surprising step, let us try to give a conceptual intuition behind this step, we will then illustrate it on a concrete example. Consider what happens in the
L_{2}
layer, assuming
L_{0}=L_{1}=\emptyset
. From states in that layer, either:

•

\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
occurs with pp;

•

otherwise,
L_{3}\cup L_{4}\cup L_{5}\cup L_{6}
occurs with pp, and the maximum index seen with pp is even;

•

otherwise, the game loops surely in
L_{2}
, and with pp the maximum color seen is
2
. In that case, Player
\mathsf{A}
wins the (real) parity game almost-surely.

In other words, either the game loops in
L_{2}
and Player
\mathsf{A}
wins, or what happens is alike to what happens in the layer
L_{4}
. In Figure
2
without dotted state
q_{1}
where Player
\mathsf{B}
plays alone,
L_{4}=\{q_{4}\}
, then
L_{3}=\{q_{3}\}
, then
L_{2}=\{q_{2}\}
, but if the play stays in
L_{2}
, Player
\mathsf{A}
wins, so Player
\mathsf{B}
may just as well go to
q_{4}
since there is no
L_{0},L_{1}
below to escape. However, in Figure
2
with dotted state
q_{1}
, Player
\mathsf{B}
can leave
L_{2}
via
L_{1}\neq\emptyset
, avoid color
4
, and win. There
L_{2}
and
L_{4}
are not alike. Hence, we do not merge them.

9.

Earlier, some states of color
1
may have been added to
L_{3}
since
L_{3}
”overruled”
L_{2}
w.r.t indices, but since
L_{2}
has just been merged into
L_{4}
, it now overrules
L_{3}
, so some states from
L_{3}
may have to go back to
L_{1}
. Therefore we reset the layers below
L_{4}
and repeat the above attractor alternation all over again, until all the states are eventually in
L_{6}
as we shall prove.

The key property that is growing throughout the above computation and will hold in the final
L_{6}
involves layer games: the
L_{n}
-game is derived from the
u
-slice by abstracting each
L_{i}
with
i\neq n
via one state
k_{i}^{n}
from which the player who dislikes the parity of
n
chooses any next state in
L_{n}
, making it harder for the other to win. If
i>n
then
k_{i}^{n}
is
i
-colored, else
(n-1)
-colored, also making it harder for the other to win. And states in
L_{n}
bear their true colors. See for instance Figure
5
. The
L_{n}
-game is only seemingly harder to win: it is actually equivalently hard, but its useful properties are easier to prove.

The key growing property is as follows: between two merges, the attractor computation from the top layer down to
L_{n}
ensures that Player
\mathsf{A}
has a positional strategy of value at least
u
in each
L_{i}
for even
i\geq n
, and Player
\mathsf{B}
less than
u
for odd
i\geq n
. In the very end, there is only one even layer with all states bearing their true colors, and no abstract states, so the last layer game equals the
u
-slice game, for which we have thus computed a positional optimal strategy.

Let us hint at how to show positional optimality in the
L_{n}
-games when it holds: we break
L_{n}
each into one simple parity game built on
\mathsf{F}(q)
per state
q
in
L_{n}
, abstracting the other states in
L_{n}
into one. Our theorem assumption yields an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy for Player
\mathsf{A}
or
\mathsf{B}
in the simple parity game. Gluing them does the job.

Now recall the above example of computation, where Player
\mathsf{A}
for even indices, and Player
\mathsf{B}
for odd ones, guarantees (assuming neither
\mathsf{Leak}_{\geq u}
nor
\mathsf{Leak}_{<u}
occurs):

•

in
L_{6}
, that a next state is of color 6 with pp;

•

in
L_{5}
, that the next state is surely not of color 6, and with pp a next state is of color 5;

•

in
L_{4}
, that a next state is of color 6 with pp or surely the next state is not of color 5, and with pp a next state is of color 4;

•

in
L_{3}
, that the next state is surely not of color 6 and that the next state is of color 5 with pp or surely the next state is not of color 4, and with pp a next state is of color 3; etc.

One can realize that the furthest the layer is from the maximal color (that is 6), the more complex the requirement is at that layer. That is why the strength of our assumption on the game form induced at some state increases with the difference between maximal true color in the
u
-slice and the true color (at most
n
) of the state, as stated in Remark
26
.
We will discuss this further in the next chapter. In particular, we will show that there is an infinite strict hierarchy between these assumptions.

6.2
Extracting an environment function from a parity game

Once restricted to the game
\mathcal{G}^{u}
, the method that we use to prove Theorem
25

consists in iteratively building a pair of (virtual) coloring
and environment functions ensuring a nice property (namely

faithfulness
, defined below in
Definition
42
). For the remainder of this section, we illustrate the definitions and lemmas on the game depicted in Figures
4
and
4
.

Figure 3:
The depiction of a game restricted to the
1/2
-slice
Q_{1/2}
with the initial coloring function
\mathsf{col}
.

Figure 4:
The same game restricted to
Q_{1/2}
with a different coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}
.

Example 29
.

We explain the notations used to depict this game (it is in
fact the same arena in both Figures
4

and
4
, with different coloring functions – real or virtual). On the sides in green are the slices
Q_{0},Q_{1/4},Q_{3/4}
and
Q_{1}
from left to right. We focus on the central slice
Q_{1/2}
. In
Q_{1/2}
, there are seven states, five of which (the square-shaped ones) are turn-based for Player
\mathsf{B}
, that is, Player
\mathsf{A}
has only one available action. On the other hand, the two circled-shaped states
{q}_{0}
and
{q}_{5}
are “truly” concurrent in the sense that both players have several actions available. Furthermore, note that only one stochastic outcome (of a local interaction) is drafted as a black dot: from
{q}_{4}
, Player
\mathsf{B}
may either loop on
{q}_{4}
or go with equal probability to
{q}_{0}
and
{q}_{6}
. The other arrows lead to a single state and the outcomes of the game forms in
{q}_{0}
or
{q}_{5}
is a single state or a value:
1
or
1/2
. These formally refer to a (distribution over) stopping states outside of the
1/2
-slice
Q_{1/2}
. The horizontal layers depict the colors of the states. In Figure
4
, the coloring function considered is the initial one
\mathsf{col}
whereas in Figure
4
we have depicted a (virtual) coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}
. For instance,
\mathsf{col}({q}_{6})=3
whereas
\mathsf{col}({q}_{5})=2
. Similarly,
\mathsf{vcol}({q}_{6})=3
whereas
\mathsf{vcol}({q}_{5})=4
. Note that, in Figure
4
, the real colors (given by
\mathsf{col}
) are reminded next to some states with circled numbers. Finally, note that
e:=e_{1/2}=4
.

Given a (virtual) coloring function, we need to extract local environments from the parity game
\mathcal{G}
, which summarize how the Players see their neighboring states via the
virtual coloring function.
This is (partly) done in Definition
30
.

Definition 30

(Probability function extracted from an arena and a
(virtual) coloring function)
.

Consider a virtual coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
. For all
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we let
Q_{n}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
. We then define the map
p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}:Q\rightarrow Q_{n}\uplus K^{n}\uplus V_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}
such that, for all
q\in Q
:

•

if
q\in Q_{n}
,
p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}(q):=q\in Q
;

•

if
q\in Q_{u}\setminus Q_{n}
,
p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}(q):=k^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}(q)}\in K^{n}
;

•

if
q\in Q\setminus Q_{u}
,
p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}(q):=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)\in V_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}
.

Given a (virtual) coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and a color
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we can now extract a smaller parity game from
\mathcal{G}
where the states with non-trivial game forms are the states in
\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
, the states in
Q\setminus Q_{u}
are stopping states and the arena loops back to
\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
when a state in
Q_{u}\setminus\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
is seen. This is done below in Definition
31
.

Definition 31

(Parity game extracted from the
u
-slice)
.

Consider a virtual coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and a color
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
. Let
\mathsf{C}\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\}
be a Player:
\mathsf{A}
if
n
is odd and
\mathsf{B}
if
n
is even. The arena
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{vcol}}^{n}=\langle Q^{\prime},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}\rangle
along with the coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}_{n}:Q^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
are such that, denoting
Q_{n}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
:

•

Q^{\prime}:=Q_{n}\cup K^{n}\cup V_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}
where all
x\in V_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}
are stopping states with
\mathsf{val}(x)\leftarrow x
;

•

for all
q\in Q_{n}
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(q):=\mathsf{F}(q)^{p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{q},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{q},Q^{\prime},\varrho_{p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}}\rangle
where, for all
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{q}
,
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{q}
, and
q\in Q
, we have
\varrho_{p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})(q):=\varrho(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})[p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{-1}[q]]
;

•

for all
k\in K^{n}
, we set
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}(k)
as a Player-
\mathsf{C}
state whose outcomes are all the states in
Q_{n}
;

•

for all
q\in Q_{n}
, we let
\mathsf{vcol}_{n}(q):=\mathsf{col}(q)
and for all
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we have
\mathsf{vcol}_{n}(k_{i}^{n}):=\max(i,n-1)
.

For
t\in[0,1]
, we define the valuation
v_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{t}:Q^{\prime}\rightarrow[0,1]
:
v_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{t}[Q_{n}\cup K^{n}]:=\{t\}
and for all
x\in V_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}
,
v_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{t}(x):=x
.

The game
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{vcol}}^{n}
is then equal to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{vcol}}^{n}=\langle\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}},\mathsf{vcol}_{n}\rangle
.

Remark 32
.

First, the notation
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
comes from the fact that
the game is extracted for the
n
-colored layer w.r.t. the coloring
function
\mathsf{vcol}
. The idea behind
Definition
31
is the following:
the states of interest are those of
Q_{n}
, that is, those for which
the virtual color given by
\mathsf{vcol}
is
n
. Note however that the
colors of these states in
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
are given by the real
coloring function
\mathsf{col}
. On the other hand, for all
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, the state
k_{i}^{n}
in
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
corresponds to the states in
\mathcal{G}^{u}
colored with
i
w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}
. In the case where
n
is even, as formally defined later in Definition
40
, we will require that any Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy generated by a given environment has value at least
u
, in the game
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
, from all states in
Q_{n}
. However, all states
k^{n}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
are Player-
\mathsf{B}
’s, who can then choose to loop back to any state in
Q_{n}
. Therefore, given a Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
, if the game cannot exit to any stopping state, for the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
not to have value 0, the game may loop on some
k_{i}^{n}
only at the condition that the highest color seen with positive probability is even.
In addition, note that the color of the state
k^{n}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
is
n-1
(which is odd). Hence, all other things being equal, the game is harder for Player
\mathsf{A}
when
n=4
than when
n=2
or
0
. Finally, note that, when
n
is odd, we will require that any Player-
\mathsf{B}
positional strategy generated by a given environment has value less than
u
.

\mathcal{L}^{3}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
. For

k_{0}^{3},k_{1}^{3},k_{2}^{3},k_{3}^{3}
and
k_{4}^{3}
are not
depicted: they would all loop back to both
{q}_{4}

and
{q}_{6}
.
