.

Example 33
.

The game
\mathcal{L}^{3}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
is partly depicted in
Figure
5
(the virtual coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}
being the one depicted in
Figure
4
). The colors of the states are
depicted in red. Although the arrows are not depicted, from all states
k_{0}^{3},k_{1}^{3},k_{2}^{3},k_{3}^{3}
and
k_{4}^{3}
Player
\mathsf{A}
can decide to which state among
\{q_{4},q_{6}\}
to loop back (since
n=3
is odd). In an even-colored layer, it would have been Player
\mathsf{B}
to decide.

Given a virtual coloring function, we also associate a local environment with each state.

Definition 34

(Local environment induced by a virtual coloring function
and a color)
.

Consider a state
q\in Q_{u}
and a coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
. We define
p_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}:Q\rightarrow\{q_{\mathsf{init}}\}\cup K_{e}\cup[0,1]
similarly to how we define
p_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}
in Definition
30
. That is, for all
q^{\prime}\in Q
, we have:

•

if
q^{\prime}=q
,
p_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}:=q_{\mathsf{init}}
;

•

if
q^{\prime}\in Q_{u}\setminus\{q\}
,
p_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}:=k_{\mathsf{vcol}(q^{\prime})}\in K_{e}
;

•

if
q^{\prime}\in Q\setminus Q_{u}
,
p_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}(q):=\chi_{\mathcal{G}}(q)\in[0,1]
.

Then, for all
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, the environment

E^{n}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
associated with state
q
w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}

and
n
is such that

E^{n}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}:=\langle\max(c_{n},\mathsf{vcol}(q)),e,p_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}\rangle
where
c_{n}=n+1
if
n
is odd
and
c_{n}:=n-1
if
n
is even. We say that the coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}
is
associated
with the environment

E^{n}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
.

The corresponding (local) game

\mathcal{G}_{(\mathsf{F}(q),E^{n}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}})}
(see
Definition
16
) is denoted

\mathcal{G}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}^{n}
. For all
x\in[0,1]
, we set

v_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}^{x}:=v^{x}_{(\mathsf{F}(q),E^{0}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}})}
(see
Definition
16
).

The definition of
c_{n}
may seem ad hoc. We give an explanation of this definition below in the next subsection.

Example 35
.

The game
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{q_{5},\mathsf{col}}
is depicted on the right of Figure
1
for
n=0,1,2
. However, if
n=3
, the color of
q_{\mathsf{init}}
would be 4, and if
n=4
, it would be 3. The game
\mathcal{G}^{n}_{q_{0},\mathsf{vcol}}
is depicted in Figure
6
for
n=0
. However, if
n=1
, the color of
q_{\mathsf{init}}
would be 2, if
n=2
, the color would be 1, if
n=3
, the color would be 4 and if
n=4
the color would be 3.

6.3
Local Operator

We want to define a way to update a (virtual) coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}
. This will be done via a local operator mapping a given
state
q
to the best color
k
for which Player
\mathsf{A}
can achieve the
value
u
in the corresponding local parity game

\mathcal{G}^{k}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
. Note that “best” is to be understood considering an ordering
compatible with the parity objective. Specifically, taking the
point-of-view of Player
\mathsf{A}
, any even number is better than any odd
number, and when they increase, odd numbers get worse whereas
even numbers get better. This induces a new ordering.

Definition 36

(Parity order)
.

We define a total strict order relation
\prec_{\mathsf{par}}
on
\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
m\prec_{\mathsf{par}}n
if:
m
is odd and
n
is even; or
m>n
and
m
and
n
are odd; or
m<n
and
m
and
n
are even.

Definition 37

(Local operator)
.

Consider a state
q\in Q_{u}
and a (possibly virtual) coloring
function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
. The color

\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})\in\mathbb{N}
induced by
\mathsf{vcol}
at
q
is defined by:

\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol}):=\max_{\prec_{\mathsf{par}}}\{n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket\mid\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}^{n}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})\geq u\}

The meaning of a new virtual color
n
assigned to a state
q
via

\mathsf{NewCol}
is the following: in the game

\mathcal{G}^{u}
with the coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}
, from state
q
and in at most one
step, the highest color w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}
seen with positive
probability when both players play optimally is
n
(and no
stopping state is seen).

Let us now explain the choice of
c_{n}
in
Definition
34
. In a local
environment parameterized by
n
, the integer
n
induces a shifted
parity objective for Player
\mathsf{A}
: her objective is that the maximal
color seen infinitely often is at least
n

w.r.t.
\prec_{\mathsf{par}}
; in particular
n=0
induces the usual
parity objective. The value
c_{n}
encodes that winning
condition. For instance, if
n=2
, assuming
\mathsf{vcol}(q)=0
for
simplicity, then
c_{n}=1
, which implies that seeing
0
infinitely
often is not enough, but seeing
2
infinitely often is enough to
win. Similarly, if
n=1
,
c_{n}=2
, which implies that seeing
1
infinitely
often is now enough to win, but seeing
3
infinitely often is still losing.

Remark 38
.

Assume for instance that
\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})=2
for some state
q\in Q
and some (virtual) coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}
2
2

2

In
particular, it must be the case that
\mathsf{vcol}(q)\leq 2
, see
Proposition
66
.
. In particular, Player
\mathsf{A}
has a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F},E^{2}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}})
, which yields a value at least
u
, and Player
\mathsf{B}
has a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy

\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F},E^{4}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}})
, which yields
a value less than
u
. Remember that all games

\mathcal{G}^{k}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
(where
k
ranges over
\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
) share the same
structure and that only the color of
q_{\mathsf{init}}
changes.
Consider what happens in the game
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
(where the state

q_{\mathsf{init}}
is colored by
\mathsf{vcol}(q)
) when playing strategies

\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
. Recalling
Remark
22
, the game cannot exit to
any stopping state since the expected value of the stopping states reached
would be both at least
u
(since
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is optimal in

\mathcal{G}^{2}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
) and less than
u
(since
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
is
optimal in
\mathcal{G}^{4}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
); hence this cannot
happen. Furthermore, if some color of value at least

3
is seen with positive probability, then the highest such color
must be both even (since
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is optimal in

\mathcal{G}^{2}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
) and odd (since
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
is optimal in

\mathcal{G}^{4}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
). In fact, the highest color seen with positive
probability in
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{q,\mathsf{vcol}}
under
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
is
2
and neither of the players can do better
(w.r.t. the ordering

\prec_{\mathsf{par}}
). From this, we can infer the
semantics of a virtual color
n
assigned to a state
q
via operator
\mathsf{NewCol}

(i.e. a color given by a virtual coloring function): in the game

\mathcal{G}^{u}
with coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}
, from state
q
and in at most one
step, the highest color w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}
seen with positive probability is
n
(and no stopping state can be seen).

Let us exemplify this operator on an example.

Example 39
.

First, consider Figure
1
and let us compute
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{5},\mathsf{col})
. We can realize that, regardless of the color of state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
, Player
\mathsf{A}
can (positionally) play both rows with positive probability and ensure reaching (almost-surely) the stopping state
1/2
. In fact, for all
n\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
, we have
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{q_{5},\mathsf{col}}^{n}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})=1/2
. Hence,
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{5},\mathsf{col})=4
.

Consider now Figure
6
and let us compute
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{0},\mathsf{vcol})
. As mentioned in Example
35
, the game
\mathcal{G}^{4}_{q_{0},\mathsf{vcol}}
corresponds to the game depicted in Figure
6
except that
q_{\mathsf{init}}
is colored with 3. One can realize that, with this choice (of coloring of the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
), if the highest color
i\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
such that
k_{i}
is seen infinitely often is such that
i\prec_{\mathsf{par}}4
, then Player
\mathsf{A}
loses. The value of this game is 0 as Player
\mathsf{B}
can ensure looping on
k_{0}
and
q_{\mathsf{init}}
(by playing, positionally and deterministically, the middle column) thus ensuring that the highest color seen infinitely often is 3. Thus,
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{0},\mathsf{vcol})\prec_{\mathsf{par}}4
. In the game
\mathcal{G}^{2}_{q_{0},\mathsf{vcol}}
,
q_{\mathsf{init}}
is colored with 1. Again, with this choice (of coloring of the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
), if the highest color
i\in\llbracket 0,4\rrbracket
such that
k_{i}
is seen infinitely often is such that
i\prec_{\mathsf{par}}2
, then Player
\mathsf{A}
loses. The value of this game is also 0 as Player
\mathsf{B}
can still play the middle column ensuring that the highest color seen infinitely often is 1. Thus,
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{0},\mathsf{vcol})\prec_{\mathsf{par}}2
. Consider now the game
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{q_{0},\mathsf{vcol}}
, the one depicted in Figure
6
. The value of the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
is now 1. Indeed, if Player
\mathsf{A}
plays the two rows with equal probability, one can see that this strategy parity dominates (see Definition
12
) the valuation
v_{q_{0},\mathsf{vcol}}^{1}
(recall Definition
34
). Indeed, the BSCCs compatible with this strategy are
\{q_{\mathsf{init}},k_{3},k_{4}\}
and
\{q_{\mathsf{init}},k_{0}\}
and they are even-colored. Hence, by Theorem
13
,
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{q_{1},\mathsf{col}}^{0}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})=1\geq 1/2
and
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{0},\mathsf{vcol})\succeq_{\mathsf{par}}0
. That is,
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{0},\mathsf{vcol})=0
.

Some of the properties enjoyed by the local operator
\mathsf{NewCol}
are given in the appendix in Page
D.1
.

6.4
Faithful coloring function

To prove Theorem
25
, we iteratively build
a (virtual) coloring function and a local
environment. We want to define the desirable property that the pair of coloring and
environment functions should satisfy that will be preserved step by
step.
First, we need to define the notion of an environment function witnessing a color.

Definition 40

(Environment witnessing a color)
.

Consider a coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, a color
n\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and an environment function

\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
with

Q_{n}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
.

Assume that
n
is even. We say that the pair

(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})

witnesses
the color

n
if for all
q\in Q_{n}
,
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathsf{Ev}(q))\leq e-\mathsf{col}(q)

and all positional Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}}
generated by
\mathsf{Ev}
(recall
Definition
24
) in the game

\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
parity dominate the valuation
v_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{u}
(recall
Definition
31
).

Assume that
n
is odd. We say that the pair
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
n
, if for all
q\in Q_{n}
,
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathsf{Ev}(q))\leq o-\mathsf{col}(q)
and for all positional Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}}
generated by
\mathsf{Ev}
in the game
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
, there is some
u^{\prime}<u
, such that
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
parity dominates the valuation

v_{n,\mathsf{vcol}}^{u^{\prime}}
.

Remark 41
.

The condition on the size of the environments considered along with the assumptions of Theorem
25
ensures that the quantification over the strategies generated by the environment function are not over the empty set.

This definition was hinted in
Remark
32
. Informally, it
means that, in the (virtual) games given by
\mathsf{vcol}
, in the
even-colored layers, Player
\mathsf{A}
can achieve at least what she
should be able to achieve in this
u
-slice (i.e. the value of the states is at least
u
). Whereas, in the odd-colored layers, Player
\mathsf{B}
can prevent Player
\mathsf{A}
from achieving this.

We can now define the notion of faithful pair of coloring and environment functions.

Definition 42

(Faithful pair of coloring and environment functions)
.

Consider a coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, some
n\in\llbracket 0,e+1\rrbracket
and a partial environment function
\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
defined on
\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[\llbracket n,e\rrbracket]
.
We say that
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is
faithful down to

n
if:

•

for all
k\in\llbracket n,e\rrbracket
, the pair
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses color
k
;

•

for all
q\in Q_{u}
, if
\mathsf{vcol}(q)<n
, then

\mathsf{col}(q)=\mathsf{vcol}(q)
and
\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})<n
;

If
n=0
, we say that the pair
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is
completely faithful
.

Only the first condition for faithfulness is really of interest to
us. For instance, this first condition suffices to show the crucial
proposition below. However, the second condition is used in the
proofs. (It is also helpful as it guides us in how to build a
completely faithful pair, as discussed below.) Note that, in the
proofs, we use an even stronger notion of faithfulness with a third
condition. However, we do not present it here in order not to
complexify too much the approach, and the two first conditions are
sufficient for the (informal) explanation of Theorem
25
. It
can however be found in Appendix
D.2
.

The benefit of faithful environments and coloring functions lies in
the proposition below: if all states are mapped w.r.t. the coloring
function to
e
, then the environment function guarantees the value
u
in the whole
u
-slice

Q_{u}
.

Proposition 43
.

For a coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and an
environment function
\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
, assume that
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is completely faithful and that

\mathsf{col}_{u}[Q_{u}]=\{e\}
. Then, all Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional
strategies generated by the environment function
\mathsf{Ev}
parity
dominate the valuation
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}
in the game

\mathcal{G}^{u}
.

Proof
.

This is direct from the definitions. Indeed, as
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is completely faithful, it follows that
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
e
(see Definition
42
). That is, all Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
generated by
\mathsf{Ev}
in the game
\mathcal{L}^{e}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
parity dominate the valuation
v_{e,\mathsf{vcol}}^{u}
(see Definition
40
). Since
\mathsf{vcol}[Q_{u}]=\{e\}
, both games
\mathcal{L}^{e}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
and
\mathcal{G}^{u}
are identical (see Definitions
27
and
31
). Similarly, the valuation
v_{e,\mathsf{vcol}}^{u}
is equal to the valuation
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}^{u}
(also see Definition
40
).

6.5
Computing a completely faithful pair

Given Lemma
43
, our goal
is to come up with a pair of an environment function and a coloring
function completely faithful such that all states are colored with

e
.
Let us first consider how to obtain a completely faithful pair from
the initial coloring function and the empty environment function
(i.e. no state is mapped to an environment). Note that this initial
pair of coloring and environment functions is faithful down to

e+1
. Hence, our goal is, given a pair
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})

faithful down to some
n\in\llbracket 1,e+1\rrbracket
, to build a new pair that
is faithful down to
n-1
. To do so, let us be guided by the second
property for faithfulness: to be faithful down to
n-1
, no state

q\in Q_{u}
such that
\mathsf{vcol}(q)\leq n-2
should be such that

\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})=n-1
. Hence, the idea is, for all such states

q\in Q_{u}
, to change their colors to
n-1
until no state

q\in Q_{u}
with
\mathsf{vcol}(q)\leq n-2
satisfies
3
3

3

This can seen as computing the “probabilistic attractor with leaks towards the stopping states” that we mentioned above.

\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})=n-1
. The environment associated to each such
state
q
newly colored by
n-1
will be given by the coloring
function
\mathsf{vcol}
for which
\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})=n-1
for the
first time (crucially, this is done before the color of
q
is updated
to

n-1
).
The procedure we have described is formally given in the
Appendix as Algorithm
15
.
Interestingly, the update done in the algorithm
preserves the faithfulness of environment and coloring functions.

Lemma 44

(Proof Appendix
D.4
, Page
D.4
)
.

Consider a coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
,

n\in\llbracket 1,e+1\rrbracket
, and a partial environment function

\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
defined on

\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[\llbracket n,e\rrbracket]
. Assume that
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is
faithful down to
n
. Let

(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{UpdateColEnv}(n-1,\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
be the pair computed by
Algorithm
15
for index
n-1
. (Only states
q
such that
\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}(q)=n-1
may have
changed their colors and be newly mapped to an environment.)
Then,
(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime})
is faithful
down to
n-1
.

Before giving a proof sketch of this lemma, let us illustrate it on an example.

Example 45
.

Let us illustrate this algorithm on Figures
4

and
4
. The first step is to build a pair that
is faithful down to
e=4
. As mentioned above in
Example
39
, we have
\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{5},\mathsf{col})=4
. Hence, the color of this state is changed to
4
(we obtain a
virtual coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}}
) and we set

\mathsf{Ev}(q_{5}):=E^{4}_{q_{5},\mathsf{col}}
. Note that a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is optimal in this environment if and
only if it plays both rows with positive probability. Furthermore,
note that, in the extracted game
\mathcal{L}^{4}_{\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}}}
, a
Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy playing such a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy

\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
in
q_{5}
parity dominates the valuation

v_{Q_{4},\mathsf{col}^{q_{5}}}^{1/2}
. Hence, the pair

(\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}},\mathsf{Ev})
is faithful down to
4
.

Consider now the layer
3
. First, the state
q_{6}
already has color

3
, so it only remains to set its environment:

\mathsf{Ev}(q_{6}):=E^{3}_{q_{6},\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}}}
. We then realize that

\mathsf{NewCol}(q_{4},\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}})=3
. Indeed,
q_{4}
is colored with
2

and may go with equal probability to a state colored with
0
and to
a state colored with
3
. The color of this state is therefore
changed, thus obtaining a new virtual coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5},q_{6},q_{4}}
. We set its environment:

\mathsf{Ev}(q_{4}):=E^{3}_{q_{4},\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}}}
. One can realize that the
pair
(\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5},q_{6},q_{4}},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
3

: a positional
Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy generated by this environment would be so that
(i) from
q_{6}
, it goes to
q_{4}
with probability
1
(to avoid

k_{4}
that is colored with
4
) and (ii) from
q_{5}
, it goes to

q_{6}
with positive probability (to see the color
3
with positive
probability). Such a strategy has value
0
in the game

\mathcal{L}^{3}_{\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5}}}=\mathcal{L}^{3}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
from
Figure
5
, hence the pair

(\mathsf{vcol}^{q_{5},q_{6},q_{4}},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
3
.

We illustrate on this step why the environment needs to be set
before changing the new color and not after. That is, we explain why
it would not be correct to set

\mathsf{Ev}(q_{4}):=E^{3}_{q_{4},\mathsf{col}^{q_{5},q_{6},q_{4}}}
instead of
what we do above.
In this environment, the state
q_{4}
has color
3
. Hence, looping
with probability
1
on
q_{4}
is an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy for
Player
\mathsf{B}
w.r.t.

(\mathsf{F}(q_{4}),\mathsf{Ev}(q_{4}))
. Then, the
corresponding pair of coloring and environment functions would not
witness the color
3
. Indeed, a Player
\mathsf{B}
strategy that loops
with probability
1
on
q_{4}
is generated by this environment, and
it has value
1\geq u
(because the real color of this state is

2
, and not
3
).

This process is then repeated down to
0
. In
Figure
4
, the depicted coloring function (with
the appropriate environment function that is not shown in
Figure
4
) are in fact completely faithful (this
is what outputs Algorithm
15
on the
coloring function of Figure
4
).

We give a proof sketch of Lemma
44
,
which explains the ideas for the first phase of the procedure for
computing a first completely faithful pair, before
Algorithm
16
is called – which we will discuss right after.

Proof

(Proof sketch)
.

We want to prove that the pair
(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime})
witnesses the
color
n-1
(the other condition for faithfulness is ensured by the
construction). We consider the case where
n-1
is even, the
other case is similar (but one needs to take the point-of-view of
Player
\mathsf{B}
). Consider a Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}

generated by the environment function
\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime}
in the game

\mathcal{L}^{n-1}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}}
.
Let
Q_{n-1}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime-1}[n-1]
and let

v:=v_{n-1,{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}}^{u}
. For every
q\in Q_{n-1}
, let

Y_{q}:=(\mathsf{F}(q),\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime}(q))
be the
local environment at state
q
and let us denote
\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime}(q)
by
\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime}(q)=\langle c_{q},e,p_{q}\rangle
. From the characterization of
Lemma
21
(item (ii.1)),
by carefully analyzing the links between the local games
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{q}}

for all
q\in Q_{n-1}

and the game
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}}
, we deduce that the
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
dominates the valuation
v
.

It remains to show that all BSCCs (that are not reduced to a stopping
state and are) compatible with
s_{\mathsf{A}}
are even-colored. Consider
such a BSCC
H
and a Player-
\mathsf{B}
deterministic positional strategy

\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
which induces
H
. For every state
q\in H
, since no stopping
state occurs in
H
, it must be that the probability to reach a
stopping state is
0
. That is, it amounts to have

\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathsf{F}(q),\mathbbm{1}_{Q\setminus Q_{u}}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)=0
.
For every state
q\in Q_{n-1}
, the coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}_{q}

associated with environment
\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime}(q)
is such that

\mathsf{vcol}_{q}(q)\leq n-1
.
4
4

4

This is because all states

q\in Q_{n-1}
satisfy
\mathsf{col}(q)\leq n-1
. This is
one of the additional conditions for faithfulness that we did
mention, but that is used in the Appendix in Definition
71
.
Hence, the color
c_{q}

is such that

c_{q}=\max(n-2,\mathsf{vcol}_{q}(q))\leq n-1
. Now, assume that some
state
k_{i}
is in
H
for some
i>n-1\geq c_{q}
. In that case,
as explained in Remark
22
, the
highest
i
such that
k_{i}
is in
H
must be even. Hence,
H
is
even-colored. Assume now that no state
k_{i}
in
H
is such that

i>n-1
. In that case, if a state in
H
has color
n-1
(like the
state
q_{6}
in Figure
4
in the case where

n-1=3
), then
n-1
is the highest color in
H
and
H
is
even-colored. Consider the first state
q
whose color is now
n-1

(w.r.t.
{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}
) but whose previous color was not
n-1
.
In that case, we have
c_{q}=\max(n-2,\mathsf{vcol}_{q}(q))=n-2
is
odd. Furthermore, the state
q
has changed its color because

\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol}_{q})=n-1
.
With Remark
22
, since
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)

is optimal w.r.t.
Y_{q}
, it follows that there is a positive
probability to reach, in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{q}}
the state
k_{n-1}
. In
the game
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}}
, this corresponds to a positive
probability to reach a state
q^{\prime}\in H
colored with
n-1

w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}_{q}
(recall
Definition
30
). Since
q

is the first state to have changed its color, we can deduce that

q^{\prime}
already had color
n-1
w.r.t.
\mathsf{vcol}
. Furthermore, one can show
that
q^{\prime}
is colored with
n-1
w.r.t. the real coloring function

\mathsf{col}
. Overall, in the game
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}}
, with
the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)
, there is a positive probability to
reach in one step a state
q^{\prime}
colored with

n-1
. Iteratively, we obtain that, considering the
k
-th state whose
color is now
n-1
(i.e. w.r.t.
{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime}}
) but whose initial
color was not
n-1
, there is a positive probability to reach (in at
most
k
steps) a state colored with
n-1
. Hence, the highest color
appearing in
H
is
n-1
, which is even. We obtain that
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}

parity dominates the valuation
v
.

Overall, applying iteratively Algorithm
15
on
all colors from
e
down to
0
starting with the initial coloring
function induces a completely faithful pair

(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
. However, it may be the case that some states
are mapped to an odd number, which does not allow to apply
Lemma
43
. The question is then: from that completely faithful configuration, how
can one make some progress towards a situation where
Lemma
43
can be applied?

Example 46
.

Consider the coloring function of Figure
4
. As
mentioned in Example
45
, with an appropriate
environment function (that is not shown in
Figure
4
), we can have a pair which is
completely faithful. To gain some intuition on what should be done next, let us focus
only on the states
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
. A simplified version is presented
in Figure
7
(with a slight
modification: instead of going to
q_{0}
,
q_{1}
loops on itself): the
initial (and true) colors of the states are in circles next to them
and their color w.r.t. the current (virtual) coloring function (that
is completely faithful with an appropriate environment function) is
written in red. In this game, Player
\mathsf{B}
plays alone, but it is obvious that Player

\mathsf{A}
wins surely from
q_{2}
: indeed, either the game stays
indefinitely in
q_{2}
, or it eventually reaches and settles in

q_{1}
.

The current virtual color
1
assigned to both
q_{2}
and
q_{3}

does not properly reflect the fact that if the game reaches
q_{3}
,
even though Player
\mathsf{B}
plays optimally according to the local
game associated with
q_{2}
, it will end up looping in
q_{1}
, which
will be losing for Player
\mathsf{B}
. In a way, we would like to
propagate the information that reaching
q_{1}
is bad for Player

\mathsf{B}
. Since
0
is the smallest color, there is no harm in
increasing it to
2
, the game from
q_{1}
will be the same: it
will be won by Player
\mathsf{A}
by looping. Player
\mathsf{B}
will now be
able to know that going to
q_{1}
is dangerous for him, which will
be obtained by applying the previous iterative process.

In a more general concurrent game, the next step of the process
when we have a completely faithful configuration not satisfying
the assumptions of
Lemma
43

consists in changing all the states with the least (virtual) color

n
to the color
n+2
.
However, note that there is a (very important) second step: the
colors of all states (virtually) colored with
n+1
should be reset
to their initial colors. The reason why can be seen again in
Figure
7
. After the color of
q_{1}

becomes
2
, the color of
q_{3}
will also become
2
. However, if
the color of the state
q_{2}
is not reset, then it is not going to
change since Player
\mathsf{B}
can choose to loop to
q_{2}
and see the color
1

for ever (in game
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{q_{2},\mathsf{vcol}}
). That is, from Player
\mathsf{B}
’s perspective, looping
indefinitely on
q_{2}
is winning, which is not what happens in the
real game (i.e. the coloring function does not faithfully describes
what happens in the game). The changes made to the coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}
from Figure
4
can be seen in
Figure
8
. Note that the process
of increasing the colors of some states by
2
can only be done with
the
least
color (otherwise faithfulness will not be
preserved).

0
0
q_{1}
1
0
q_{2}
1
1
q_{3}

Figure 7:
A (deterministic turn-based) game with only three states.

Figure 8:
The same arena as in Figures
4
,
4
but with a different coloring function.

The process described in
Example
46
is implemented as
Algorithm
16
in the Appendix,
it ensures the lemma below.

Lemma 47

(Proof Appendix
D.5
, Page
D.5
)
.

Let

\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
,

\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
be a coloring and an environment functions. Let
n:=\min\mathsf{vcol}[Q]
. Assume that
n\leq e-2
and the pair
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is completely faithful. If
(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{IncLeast}(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is the result of
increasing the least-colored layer by 2 and resetting the environment of the last but least-colored layer (Algorithm
16
), then

(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ev}^{\prime})
is faithful down to
n+2
.

Proof

(Proof sketch)
.

Let
Q_{n}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n]
and

Q_{n+2}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[n+2]
. The algorithm has three steps:
first, it increases the least color by
2
;
then it resets the environments of the
(n+1)
-colored
states; finally it applies Algorithm
14
to these reset states. Let us argue that
(\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{Ev})
(obtained
after the first step) witnesses the color
n+2
.

Consider a Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
generated by the
environment
\mathsf{Ev}
in the game
\mathcal{L}^{n+2}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime\prime}}}
. Let

v:=v^{u}_{n+1,\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime\prime}}
. Similarly to the proof of Lemma

44
,
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
dominates the
valuation
v
.
Consider a BSCC
H
compatible with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
. If

H\cap Q_{n+2}=\emptyset
, then
H
is even-colored. Indeed,

(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
n
. In addition, the
probability to go to a state
k_{i}^{n+2}
that is
(n+1)
-colored in the game

\mathcal{L}^{n+2}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime\prime}}}
is exactly the probability to
go to a state
k_{i}^{n}
that is
(n+1)
-colored in the game
\mathcal{L}^{n}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
(since
n
is the least color). Furthermore,
H
is also even-colored as soon as
H\cap Q_{n}=\emptyset
since
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color
n+2
. Now, assume that
none of these cases occur.
Then, one can show that: either a state
k_{i}
is seen for some

i\geq n+2
, and
H
is even-colored; or, from some states in

Q_{n+2}
, there is a positive probability to exit
Q_{n+2}
and no
state
k_{i}
is seen for
i\geq n+2
. Now, looking at what happens
in game
\mathcal{L}^{n+2}_{{\mathsf{vcol}}}
, some states
k_{i}
are
seen for
i\leq n+1
, and such states are colored with

n+1
. Hence, since
(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
witnesses the color

n+2
, it must be that the highest color in
H
is
n+2
, which is
even. Therefore it is also the case in the game

\mathcal{L}^{n+2}_{{\mathsf{vcol}^{\prime\prime}}}
. In all the cases,
H
is even-colored.

As stated in Lemma
47
, the update of colors described in Example
46
can be done only if, for a completely faithful pair, the least (virtual) color
n
appearing is at most
e-2
. If
n=e
, we are actually in the scope of Lemma
43
since in that case all states have (virtual) color
e
. However, there remains the case where we have
n=e-1
. In fact, this case cannot happen.

Lemma 48

(Proof Appendix
D.6
, Page
D.6
)
.

Consider a coloring function

\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, an
environment function

\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
. Assume that

(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
is completely faithful. Then, for

C:=\mathsf{vcol}[Q]
, we have
\min C\neq e-1
.

Proof

(Proof sketch)
.

Let
Q_{e-1}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[e-1]
. Towards a contradiction, let
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
be a Player-
\mathsf{B}
positional strategy generated by

\mathsf{Ev}
in the game
\mathcal{L}^{e-1}_{\mathsf{vcol}}
. It
parity dominates the valuation
v_{e-1,\mathsf{vcol}}^{u^{\prime}}
for some

u^{\prime}<u
. Hence, all BSCCs compatible with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
are odd-colored: they all stay in the layer
Q_{e-1}
. Indeed, since
e-1=\min C
, exiting
Q_{e-1}
while staying in
Q_{u}
means seeing
Q_{e}:=\mathsf{vcol}^{-1}[e]
with
e
even and the highest color in the game.
Hence, either the game stays indefinitely in
Q_{e-1}
and Player

\mathsf{B}
wins almost surely, or there is some positive probability to
visit stopping states, and in that case their expected values is at most

u^{\prime}
. Hence, in the game
\mathcal{G}^{u}
, the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
has
values less than
u
from the states
Q_{e-1}\subseteq Q_{u}
, which
is a contradiction.

Finally, all these pieces are put together in
Algorithm
17
in the Appendix, whose output is a
completely faithful pair where all states are mapped to
e
. The only remaining step is to prove the termination of this algorithm. Let us consider the (virtual) coloring functions as vectors in
\mathbb{N}^{e+1}
indicating the number of states mapped to each color. Then, one can realize that each step of Algorithm
17
increases this vector for a lexicographic order (i.e. we first compare the number of states mapped to
e
, then the number of states mapped to
e-1
,
etc). Hence, Algorithm
17
does terminate in
finitely many steps.

Lemma 49

(Proof Appendix
D.7
, Page
D.7
)
.

Algorithm
17
computes a completely faithful pair
of environment and coloring functions mapping each state to
e
in
finitely many steps.

We can now proceed to the proof of Theorem
25
.

Proof
.

Let us prove Theorem
25
for Player

\mathsf{A}
. Consider some
u\in V_{Q}\setminus\{0\}
. By
Lemma
49
, there is a completely faithful pair of
environment and coloring functions
(\mathsf{vcol}_{u},\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u})

mapping each state in
Q_{u}
to
e_{u}
. Hence, by
Lemma
43
, all
Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategies generated by the environment
function
\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u}
parity dominate the valuation
\chi_{\mathcal{G}}

in the game
\mathcal{G}^{u}
. Since we assume that all game forms occurring in

Q_{u}
are positionally maximizable up to
e_{u}-\mathsf{col}(q)

w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{A}
, such positional strategies generated by

\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u}
do exist. Then, considering the environment function

\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}:Q\rightarrow\mathsf{Ev}(\mathsf{D})
that merges all
the environment functions

(\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{A}}^{u})_{u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{0\}}
together (and that
is defined arbitrarily on
Q_{0}
), it follows by
Lemma
28
,
that all Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategies generated by that
environment function
\mathsf{Ev}
are optimal. (And such strategies
exist.)

7
Positionally optimizable game forms

In this section, we state some properties that are ensured by positionally optimizable game forms. We begin by giving some notations for positionally optimizable game forms (and the corresponding decision problems).

Notation 50
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathsf{ParO}(n)

be the set of all game forms positionally
optimizable up to
n
and we let
\mathsf{IsO}(n)

be the problem of deciding whether a
game form is in
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
. Furthermore, we let

\mathsf{ParO}:=\cap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{ParO}(n)
and we denote by

\mathsf{IsO}
the problem of deciding whether a game form is in

\mathsf{ParO}
.

7.1
Some positionally optimizable game forms

Let us first exhibit some positionally maximizable game forms. Given the distance to the highest color in the game, Theorem
25
and
Remark
20
give exactly the game forms that
should be used in parity games to ensure the existence of positional
optimal strategies for both players. The game forms in
\mathsf{ParO}

are the ones that can be used in all parity games, regardless of the
number of colors involved, while ensuring the existence of positional
optimal strategies (for both players). Such game forms do exist. For instance,
, turn-based game forms are positionally optimizable (straightforwardly). In fact, all
determined

[
2
]
game forms are positionally
optimizable. A deterministic (i.e. where all distributions over are the outcomes are deterministic) game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
is determined if it satisfies the following property: for all
\{0,1\}
-valuations of the outcomes
v:\mathsf{O}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, in the game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{F},v\rangle
, there is either a row of
1
or a column of
0
. In particular, all turn-based game forms are determined. Furthermore, all game forms with at most two outcomes are in
\mathsf{ParO}
. This is stated formally in the lemma below.

Proposition 51

(Proof
E.1
, Page
E.1
)
.

Consider a set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
and a game form
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{D},\varrho\rangle\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{D})
. Assume that
\mathcal{F}
is either determined (in particular, it may be turn-based), or such that
\mathsf{O}\leq 2
. In that case,
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}
.

7.2
Deciding if a game form is positionally optimizable

Now, we want to show that it is decidable if a game form if positionally optimizable. To do so, we are going to encode this problem in the first order of the reals, that we introduce in the subsection below.

7.2.1
First-order theory of the reals

Informally, first order theory of the reals formulas use (existential or universal) quantification over real variables, classical logical connectors and (in)equalities between multi-variate polynomials. We formally define them below.

Definition 52

(First-order theory of the reals)
.

In the first order theory of the reals (
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
for short), we consider formulas
\Phi
of the shape:

\Phi=Q_{1}\;x_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\ldots,Q_{n}\;x_{n}\in\mathbb{R},\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

where
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

•

for all
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
,
Q_{i}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
. That is, it is either an existential or a universal quantifier;

•

\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a classical formula without quantifiers, with
\wedge
(and),
\lor
(or) and
\lnot
(negation) as logical connectors. The atomic propositions considered in
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
are of the shape:

P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\bowtie 0

where
\bowtie\;\in\{=,\neq,\geq,>,\leq,<\}
and
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a real multi-variate polynomial with integer coefficients.

The semantics behind the first order theory of the reals is quite intuitive, though it would take some space to formally define. Instead, let us illustrate it on a couple of examples.

Example 53
.

Consider the
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
formula below.

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\;\exists y\in\mathbb{R},\;x+y\geq 0

This
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
formula is true since it does hold that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, taking
y:=-x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
x+y=0
. However, the
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
formula

\exists x\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall y\in\mathbb{R},\;x+y\geq 0

is false in
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
since there is no
x\in\mathbb{R}
, such that, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
x+y\geq 0
.

Let us now consider the decision problem associated with
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
formulas.

Definition 54
.

We denote by
\mathsf{True}_{\mathsf{FO}-\mathbb{R}}
the set of all first-order theory of the reals formulas that evaluate to true. The decision problem
\mathsf{Is}_{\mathsf{FO}-\mathbb{R}}
is as follows:

•

Input: an
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
formula
\Phi
;

•

Output: yes if and only if the formula
\Phi
is in
\mathsf{True}_{\mathsf{FO}-\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorem 55
.

The decision problem
\mathsf{Is}_{\mathsf{Fo}-\mathbb{R}}
is decidable.

This result was first shown in
[
15
]
. It was then improved in
[
14
, Theorem 1.1]
it as it was shown that
\mathsf{Is}_{\mathsf{FO}-\mathbb{R}}
could be decided in doubly-exponential time via a quantifier elimination procedure.

7.2.2
Encoding in the first order theory of the reals

Let us come back to the problem of interest of us: deciding if a game form is positionally optimizable. To tackle this issue, we first realize that a game form is positionally optimizable if and only if there are optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategies w.r.t. all
relevant
environments, defined below.

Definition 56

(Relevant environments)
.

For a set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
, an environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
is
relevant
if
c\in\{0,1\}
,
p^{-1}[\{c-1\}]=p^{-1}[\{q_{\mathsf{init}}\}]=\emptyset
and, for all
i\in\llbracket c,e\rrbracket
, there is
o\in\mathsf{O}
such that
p(o)=k_{i}
. The
size
of a relevant environment
E
is equal to
\mathsf{Sz}(E):=e-c
.

As mentioned above, we may only consider relevant environment to decide if a game form is positionally maximizable.

Proposition 57

(Proof Appendix
E.2
, Page
E.2
)
.

Consider a set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
and a game form

\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
. Consider a Player
\mathsf{C}\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\}
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the game form
\mathcal{F}
is positionally maximizable w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{C}
up to
n
if and only if, for all relevant environments
E
with
\mathsf{Sz}(E)\leq n-1
, there is an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy in
\mathcal{F}
for Player
\mathsf{C}
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F},E)
.

The proof of this proposition is quite long and actually uses a result that is not published yet, but that is in the PhD thesis of one of the authors, which will soon be available online.

The benefit of considering only relevant environment is that, given any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, deciding if a game form is positionally optimizable up to
l
can be done by considering environments where all the outcomes are mapped to integers at most
l
. Then, the fact that a game form is positionally optimizable can be encoded in the first-order theory of the reals.

Proposition 58
.

Consider a deterministic game form
\mathcal{F}
. The fact that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}(n)
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
) can be encoded, with formula of size polynomial in
|\mathcal{F}|
(resp. and
n
), in
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Consider some
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We encode the fact that a standard finite deterministic game form
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{O},\varrho\rangle
is positionally optimizable up to
l
. Without loss of generality, since the game form
\mathcal{F}
is deterministic, we assume that
\mathsf{O}=\varrho(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})
. To do so, we use the characterization of Proposition
57
and we express in
\mathsf{FO}
-
\mathbb{R}
the fact that, for all relevant environments
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle
of size at most
l-1
, both players have an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy. We use the characterization given by Lemma
21
. We assume that
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}=\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
,
\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}=\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and we consider the formula
\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}
below, that we will explain line by line in the following.

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}:=\forall c,

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{ZeroOrOne}(c)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\forall\alpha=(\alpha_{o})_{o\in\mathsf{O}},

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{ZeroOrOne}(\alpha)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\forall v=(v_{o})_{o\in\mathsf{O}},

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{RealBetweenZeroOne}(v)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\forall k=(k_{o})_{o\in\mathsf{O}},

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{IntBetweenZeroL}(k,c)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\exists u,

\displaystyle\;(0\leq u\leq 1)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\exists S_{\mathsf{A}}=S_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},\ldots,S_{\mathsf{A}}^{n},\;

\displaystyle\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}=\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{n},\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{IsIndicator}(S_{\mathsf{A}})\wedge\mathsf{IsStrategy}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\wedge\mathsf{IsSupp}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},S_{\mathsf{A}})\wedge\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\alpha,v,u)\wedge

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq m}\mathsf{MaxIntegerEven}(S_{\mathsf{A}},k,j,c)\;\wedge

\displaystyle\exists S_{\mathsf{B}}=S_{\mathsf{B}}^{1},\ldots,S_{\mathsf{B}}^{m},\;

\displaystyle\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}=\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}^{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}^{n},\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{IsIndicator}(S_{\mathsf{B}})\wedge\mathsf{IsStrategy}_{\mathsf{B}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})\wedge\mathsf{IsSupp}(\sigma_{\mathsf{B}},S_{\mathsf{B}})\wedge\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{B}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{B}},\alpha,v,u)\wedge

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathsf{MaxIntegerOdd}(S_{\mathsf{B}},k,i,c)

The formula
\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}
does not fit exactly the formalism of Definition
52
, since all the quantifiers are not at the beginning of the formula. However, the semantics is unchanged if these quantifiers are moved at the beginning of the formula. We presented
\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}
in that way for readability.

The first four lines encode the relevant environment
E
. Specifically:

\mathsf{ZeroOrOne}(c):=(c=0)\lor(c=0)\;

Since the environment
E
is relevant,
c
is equal to either 0 or 1. Furthermore:

\mathsf{ZeroOrOne}(\alpha):=\bigwedge_{o\in\mathsf{O}}((\alpha_{o}=0)\lor(\alpha_{o}=1))

where, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}
,
\alpha_{o}=1
means that
p(o)\in[0,1]
and
\alpha_{o}=0
means that
p(o)\in K_{l}
. Furthermore:

\mathsf{RealBetweenZeroOne}(v):=\bigwedge_{o\in\mathsf{O}}(0\leq v_{o}\leq 1)

where, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}
,
v_{o}\in[0,1]
corresponds to the value of
o
w.r.t.
p
(assuming
p(o)\in[0,1]
). Furthermore:

\mathsf{IntBetweenZeroL}(k,c):=\bigwedge_{o\in\mathsf{O}}(\bigvee_{0\leq p\leq l}k_{o}=p)\wedge((c=0)\Rightarrow(k_{o}\leq l-1))

where, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}
,
k_{o}\in\llbracket 0,l\rrbracket
is the index of
o
given by
p
(assuming that
p(o)\in K_{l}
). Furthermore, since the size of
E
is at most
l-1
, if
c=0
, then the maximum of the colors should be at most
l-1
.

Then,
u
is the value of the game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
from the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
. This is ensured by the remainder of the formula: it exhibits a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy per player whose corresponding positional strategy parity dominates the valuation
v_{Y}^{u}
for
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. The predicates are used for both players, we give them only for Player
\mathsf{A}
, the case of Player
\mathsf{B}
being analogous.

\mathsf{IsIndicator}(S_{\mathsf{A}}):=\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}((S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=0)\lor(S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1))

where
S_{\mathsf{A}}
encodes the support of the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
. The fact that it is indeed a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy is ensured by the predicates below:

\mathsf{IsStrategy}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}):=\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}((0\leq\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}\leq 1)\wedge(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1))

In addition, we have:

\mathsf{IsSupp}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},S_{\mathsf{A}}):=\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}((\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}>0)\Leftrightarrow(S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1))

This predicate ensures that
S_{\mathsf{A}}
does indeed correspond to the support of the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
. Furthermore:

\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\alpha,v,u):=\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq k}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}\cdot(\alpha_{\varrho(i,j)}\cdot v_{\varrho(i,j)}+(1-\alpha_{\varrho(i,j)})\cdot u)\geq u)

This predicates encodes the fact that the Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
dominates the valuation
v_{Y}^{u}
. Indeed, note that for all
o\in\mathsf{O}
, if
\alpha_{o}=1
, then
p(o)\in[0,1]
, and therefore
v_{Y}^{u}(o)=p(o)=v_{o}
. Similarly, if
\alpha_{o}=0
, then
p(o)\in K_{l}
, and therefore
v_{Y}^{u}(o)=u
. Finally, when
l
is even, denoting
l=2\cdot x
with
x\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{MaxIntegerEven}(S_{\mathsf{A}},k,j,c):=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq n}(S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1\wedge\alpha_{\varrho(i,j)}=1)\lor

\displaystyle\bigvee_{0\leq e\leq x}(2e\geq c)\wedge((\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq n}(S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1)\wedge(k_{\varrho(i,j)}=2e))\;\wedge

\displaystyle(\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}(S_{\mathsf{A}}^{i}=1)\Rightarrow(k_{\varrho(i,j)}\leq 2\cdot e)))

It is similar if
l
is odd, up to small changes. This predicate encodes item
(ii.2)
of Lemma
21
: for all columns
j\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}
, either there is positive probability to see an outcome mapped to a value in
[0,1]
(i.e.
\mathsf{out}{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[0,1]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},j)>0
) or the maximum of the colors in the support of the strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is even (i.e.
\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},j)\cup\{c\})
is even).

By Lemma
21
and Proposition
57
, we have that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}(l)
if and only if
\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}\in\mathsf{True}_{\mathsf{FO}-\mathbb{R}}
. Furthermore, the size of
\Phi_{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathsf{ParO}(l)}
is polynomial in the size of
\mathcal{F}
(and in
l
).

Finally, since given an environment, an outcome is mapped to at most one integer, and since in relevant environments of size
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the outcomes are mapped to at least
n-1
different integers, we have
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{ParO}(n+2)
for
n:=|\mathsf{O}|
. The proposition follows.
∎

7.3
Hierarchy

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the game forms in
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
are the ones to
be used — to ensure the existence of positional optimal strategies
for both players — in parity games at states where the gap between
the color of the state and the maximum color in the game is at most

n
. Straightforwardly,
\mathsf{ParO}(n)\subseteq\mathsf{ParO}(n+1)
. In fact,
this inclusion is strict for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This defines an infinite
hierarchy of game forms.

Proposition 59
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the set of game forms positionally optimizable up to
n
(i.e.
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
) strictly contains the set of game forms optimizable up to
n+1
(i.e.
\mathsf{ParO}(n+1)
). That is, we have
\mathsf{ParO}(n)\supsetneq\mathsf{ParO}(n+1)
.

For all
n\geq 1
, we exhibit a standard finite game form
\mathcal{F}_{n}
that is in
\mathsf{ParO}(n-1)
but not in
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
. This is done in Definition
60
below, where we describe a game form that is
\mathsf{ParO}(n)
but not in
\mathsf{ParO}(n-1)
in the case where
n
is even.

Definition 60
.

Consider some even integer
n\geq 2
. We consider the set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}_{n}:=\{x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},y,z\}
and we consider the game form
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{n},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{n},\mathsf{O}_{n},\varrho_{n}\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}
depicted in Figure
9
. Let us describe the set of actions of the players.We set:

•

\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{n}:=\{a_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{b}},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1},a_{1,0},\ldots,a_{n-1,n-2},a_{\mathsf{Ex}}\}
; and

•

\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{n}:=\{b_{\mathsf{l}},b_{\mathsf{r}},b_{0},\ldots,b_{n-2},b_{2,1},\ldots,b_{n-2,n-3},b_{\mathsf{Ex}}\}
.

The actions
a_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{b}}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{l}},b_{\mathsf{r}}
) refer to the two topmost rows (resp. leftmost columns):
a_{\mathsf{t}}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{l}}
) leads to
\frac{x_{0}+\ldots+x_{n-1}}{n},\frac{3y+z}{4}
whereas
a_{\mathsf{b}}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{r}}
) leads to
\frac{3y+z}{4},\frac{y+3z}{4}
. Then, for all odd
i\leq n-1
(resp. even
j\leq n-2
)
a_{i}
and
a_{i,i-1}
(resp.
b_{j}
and
b_{j,j-1}
— only if
j\geq 2
) correspond to the rows leading to
x_{i}
and
\frac{x_{i}+x_{i-1}}{2}
(resp. columns leading to
x_{j}
and
\frac{x_{j}+x_{j-1}}{2}
) respectively. Finally, action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
) correspond to the bottommost row (resp. rightmost column).

Figure 9:
The game form
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Due to a lack of space,
h
refers to
n-1
and
h^{\prime}
to
n-2
.

The game forms described in Definition
60
satisfies the lemma below.

Lemma 61

(Proof
E.3
, Page
E.3
)
.

Consider some even
n\geq 2
. The game form of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
Definition
60
is:

•

positionally maximizable w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{B}
;

•

positionally maximizable w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{A}
up to
n-1
but not up to
n
.

That is:
\mathcal{F}_{n}\in\mathsf{ParO}(n)\setminus\mathsf{ParO}(n-1)
.

The proof of this lemma is very tedious since it requires to consider a lot of different possibilities in terms of which outcome is mapped to which value or color. We give here an informal proof of this lemma in the case where
n=2
. The game form
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is depicted in Figure
11
.

Figure 10:
The game form
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.

Figure 11:
The game form
\mathcal{F}_{2}
in a specific environment.

Proof sketch.

Consider a relevant environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
and the corresponding parity game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}
(recall Definition
16
). We let
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
. For all
t\in\mathsf{O}_{2}
, if
p(t)\notin[0,1]
, we let
n_{t}\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
denote the integer that
t
is mapped to w.r.t.
p
, i.e. the integer ensuring
p(t)=k_{n_{t}}
.
We want to show that, in any case, Player
\mathsf{B}
has a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy that is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
and that this also holds for Player
\mathsf{A}
as long as
c=e
(i.e. if
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=0=n-2
). However, there is a relevant environment
E
with
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=1
such that Player
\mathsf{A}
has no
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
. There are several cases, we detail some of them.

•

Assume that
p(y),p(z)\notin[0,1]
. This implies
u\in\{0,1\}
and playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{A}
and action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{B}
is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
.

•

Assume now that
p(y)\in[0,1]
and
p(z)\notin[0,1]
. Then, we have
u=p(y)
. The reason is that, by playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
, Player
\mathsf{A}
ensures that almost surely a stopping state of value
p(y)
is reached. Furthermore, Player
\mathsf{B}
can ensure the same thing by playing action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
. Then, playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{A}
and action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{B}
is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
. This is similar if
p(y)\notin[0,1]
and
p(z)\in[0,1]
.

•

Assume now
p(y),p(z)\in[0,1]
. If
p(y)<p(z)
, then we have
u=\frac{3p(y)+p(z)}{4}
, and as before playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{A}
and action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
for Player
\mathsf{B}
is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
.

•

Assume now that
p(y),p(z)\in[0,1]
and
p(y)\geq p(z)
. If
p(x_{0})\in[0,1]
or
p(x_{1})\in[0,1]
, we can also exhibit an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy for both players.

•

Let us now assume that
p(y),p(z)\in[0,1]
with
p(y)\geq p(z)
. For simplicity, we assume that
p(y)=1
and
p(z)=0
. In that case, we have:

\frac{1}{4}=\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}\leq u\leq\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}=\frac{3}{4}

Assume also that
p(x_{0}),p(x_{1})\notin[0,1]
. Assume first that
n_{x_{0}}
is odd. In that case,
u=\frac{1}{4}
. A Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy playing positionally action
b_{0}
achieves this value in the game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}
. Thus, the Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
.

Assume now that
n_{x_{0}}
is even. Then, we have
u=\frac{3}{4}
: Indeed, for all
\varepsilon>0
, a Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy playing action
a_{\mathsf{t}}
with probability
1-\varepsilon
and action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
with probability
\varepsilon
has value at least
\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}
. Therefore, playing action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
is always optimal for Player
\mathsf{B}
. It will also be the case for Player
\mathsf{A}
if
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=0
, i.e. if
c=e
. Indeed, in that case, we have either
n_{x_{0}}=n_{x_{1}}=0
, in which case
u=\frac{3}{4}
and the Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
a_{1}
is optimal w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)
; or
n_{x_{0}}=n_{x_{1}}=1
, in which case we are in the scope of the previous case of this item since
n_{x_{0}}
is odd. However, if
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=1
, there may be an issue for Player
\mathsf{A}
, as we show below.

Consider now a relevant environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle
of size 1 such that
c:=0
,
p(y):=1
,
p(z):=0
,
p(x_{0})=k_{0}
and
p(x_{1})=k_{1}
. We do have
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=1
. What we obtain is depicted in Figure
11
. In that case, we have
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}_{2},E}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})=\frac{3}{4}
, as argued above in the last item. Consider any Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F}_{2})
. If it plays action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
or action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
with positive probability, then the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
does not dominate the valuation
v^{u}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}
. However, if it does not play these actions with positive probability, the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}^{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
has value 0 in
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F}_{2},E)}}
from
q_{\mathsf{init}}
. Indeed, by positionally playing action
b_{\mathsf{l}}
, Player
\mathsf{B}
ensures that: 1) surely, no stopping state is seen, and 2) almost surely, the state
k_{1}
(of color 1) is seen infinitely often, while
c=0
.
∎

Let us now consider the proof of Proposition
59
.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 1
. The inclusion
\mathsf{ParO}(n-1)\supseteq\mathsf{ParO}(n)
is direct. Lemma
61
ensures that these two sets are not equal when
n
is even. A similar lemma could be stated in the case where
n
is odd.
∎

Appendix A
Explanations on Table
1
from the introduction

Let us describe exactly what the rows and columns of the table refer to. First, the rows correspond to the objectives considered in a finite concurrent arena:

•

Safety: Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to avoid a target (i.e. a set of states);

•

Reach: stands for reachability, Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to reach a target;

•

Büchi: Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to reach a target infinitely often, it corresponds to a parity objective with colors
1
and
2
;

•

co-B: stands for co-Büchi, Player
\mathsf{A}
wants to reach a target only finitely often, it corresponds to a parity objective with colors
0
and
1
;

•

Parity: arbitrary parity objective.

Consider now the columns.

•

The first column on the left,
\exists
opt strat?, tells if there always exist optimal strategies in finite games for these objectives. This holds for the safety objective, but it does not for reachability, therefore it does not either for Büchi, co-Büchi and parity as they are more involved than the reachability objective.

•

The second column, opt, tells the simpler kind of strategies that allow to play optimally, assuming it is possible. In any cell, pos stands for positional whereas
\infty
stands for infinite memory, i.e. strategies that cannot be described with a finite automaton.

•

The third column,
\varepsilon
-opt, tells the simpler kind of strategies that allow to play
\varepsilon
-optimally. Note that such strategies always exist by definition of the value of the game.

•

Finally, the forth column, SubG-opt, tells the simpler kind of strategies that allow to play subgame optimally, assuming it is possible. A strategy is subgame optimal if all of its residual strategies are optimal. (See e.g.
[
1
]
for more details.) Note that being subgame is stronger than being optimal, however both notions coincide on positional strategies. Note that the need for infinite-memory strategies for arbitrary parity objectives already occurs with 3 colors.
