.

D.7
Proof of Lemma
49
, Page
49

First, let us formally define a way to compare coloring functions.

Definition 80
.

We define the (transitive) relation
\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}
on coloring functions which corresponds to the lexicographic order. Specifically, for all virtual coloring functions
\mathsf{vcol}_{1},\mathsf{vcol}_{2}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
, we have
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
if and only if there is some
k\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
such that:

•

for all
i\in\llbracket k+1,e\rrbracket
, we have
|\mathsf{vcol}_{1}^{-1}[i]|=|\mathsf{vcol}_{2}^{-1}[i]|
;

•

|\mathsf{vcol}_{1}^{-1}[k]|<|\mathsf{vcol}_{1}^{-1}[k]|
.

We write
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}=_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
when we have neither
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
or
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
. We also write
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}\preceq_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
for
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}=_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
or
\mathsf{vcol}_{1}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{2}
.

Straightforwardly, this relation ensures the following proposition.

Proposition 81
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. There is no infinite sequence
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket^{n})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
c_{k}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}c_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof
.

This is because the relation
\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}
corresponds to a lexicographic order on vectors taking their values in
\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
.

We can then consider all steps of Algorithm
17
one by one.

Lemma 82
.

Consider a virtual coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
and some
k\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
. For all partial environment functions
\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
defined on
\llbracket k+1,e\rrbracket
:

•

for
(\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{upd}},\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{upd}})\leftarrow\mathsf{UpdateColEnv}(k,\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
, we have
\mathsf{vcol}\preceq_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{upd}}
;

For all environment functions
\mathsf{Ev}:Q_{u}\rightarrow\mathsf{Env}(Q)
:

•

for
(\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{Inc}},\mathsf{Ev}_{\mathsf{Inc}})\leftarrow\mathsf{IncLeast}(\mathsf{vcol},\mathsf{Ev})
, we have
\mathsf{vcol}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{Inc}}
;

Proof
.

The procedure
\mathsf{UpdateColEnv}
consists in two part. First, the call Algorithm
13
. This does not change the coloring function. Then, there is the call to Algorithm
14
. By Proposition
66
, if
\mathsf{NewCol}(q,\mathsf{vcol})=k
, then
\mathsf{vcol}(q)\leq k
. Hence, the change of colors of the states does not decrease the colors of the states. It follows that the resulting coloring function
\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{upd}}
is such that
\mathsf{vcol}\preceq_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{upd}}
.

Consider now the procedure
\mathsf{IncLeast}
. By definition, before the call of this algorithm, some states are colored with
c_{\min}
. Hence, the coloring function resulting of the call to Algorithm
16
has more states colored with
c_{\min}+2
, whereas states colored with a color higher than
c_{\min}+2
are left unchanged. Hence:
\mathsf{vcol}\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}\mathsf{vcol}_{\mathsf{Inc}}
.

We can now proceed to the proof of Lemma
49
.

Proof
.

By Lemma
82
, each call to the procedure
\mathsf{IncLeast}
strictly increase (w.r.t.
\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}
) the coloring function. Furthermore, each call to the procedure
\mathsf{UpdateColEnv}
does not decrease (w.r.t.
\prec_{\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket}
) the coloring function. Hence, by Proposition
81
, the call to the procedure
\mathsf{IncLeast}
is done only finitely many times.

Appendix E
Proofs from Section
7

E.1
Proof of Proposition
51
, Page
51

First, let us formally recall the definition of deterministic and of determined game forms.

Definition 83

(Deterministic and determined game forms)
.

Consider a set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
. A game form
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{O},\varrho\rangle\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
is
deterministic
if, for all
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}})
and
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})
, the probability distribution
\varrho(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})
is deterministic.

Furthermore, the game form
\mathcal{F}\in\mathsf{Form}(\mathsf{O})
is
determined
if it is deterministic and, for all
v:\mathsf{O}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, either there is some
a\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}
such that
\varrho(a,\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})=\{1\}
or there is some
b\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}
such that
\varrho(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},b)=\{0\}
.

Proof.

In
[
2
]
, it was shown that in all locally determined concurrent games (i.e. concurrent games where game forms are determined), both players have positional optimal strategies. Hence, determined game forms are positionally optimizable.

Consider now a game form
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{D},\varrho\rangle
such that
|\mathsf{O}|=2
. Let
\mathsf{O}:=\{x,y\}
. Consider an environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle
and the induced parity game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
for
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. If
p(x),p(y)\in[0,1]
, then the game cannot loop and both players have optimal positional strategies. Assume that
p(x)\in[0,1]
and
p(y)\notin[0,1]
. If there is a row of
y
and
y
is mapped w.r.t.
p
to an even integer (if it is mapped to
q_{\mathsf{init}}
, we consider the integer
c
), then Player
\mathsf{A}
can surely win by playing the corresponding row. Similarly, if there is a column of
y
and
y
is mapped w.r.t.
p
to an odd integer, then Player
\mathsf{B}
can surely win by playing the corresponding column. Otherwise, both players can play a uniform distribution over all actions that do not only lead to
y
, thus ensuring that the value of both strategies (and of the game) is equal to
p(x)
. The reasoning is identical if
p(x)\notin[0,1]
and
p(y)\in[0,1]
. Finally, assume that
p(x),p(y)\notin[0,1]
and assume that
x
is mapped w.r.t.
p
to the highest integer among the two. If there is a row of
y
and
y
is mapped to an even integer, then Player
\mathsf{A}
can surely win by playing the corresponding row. Similarly, if there is a column of
y
and
y
is mapped to an odd integer, then Player
\mathsf{B}
can surely win by playing the corresponding column. Otherwise, one of the players wins almost surely by playing a uniform probability distribution over all actions: if
x
is mapped to an even integer, then Player
\mathsf{A}
wins almost-surely, otherwise, Player
\mathsf{B}
wins almost-surely.
∎

E.2
Proof of Proposition
57
, Page
57

We prove the result for Player
\mathsf{A}
, the arguments are similar for Player
\mathsf{B}
. In the remainder of this section, we fix a set of outcomes
\mathsf{O}
and a game form
\mathcal{F}=\langle\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{O},\varrho\rangle
.

Definition 84
.

Consider some
n\geq 1
and an (arbitrary) environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
with
p:\mathsf{O}\rightarrow\{q_{\mathsf{init}}\}\cup[0,1]\cup K_{e}
with
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)=n
. Assume that no outcome
o\in\mathsf{O}
is such that
p(o)=q_{\mathsf{init}}
. Let
\tilde{e}:=\mathsf{Even}(e)\geq 2
. By definition, we have
n=\tilde{e}-c
. Let us define a relevant environment
E^{\prime}=\langle c^{\prime},e^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
of size
n-1
.

We let:

c^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }c\text{ is even }\\
1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

We also let
\mathsf{Im}_{p}:=\{n\in\llbracket c+1,\tilde{e}-1\rrbracket\mid\exists o\in\mathsf{O},\;p(o)=k_{i}\}
and
c=a_{0}<a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<a_{k}
be such that
\mathsf{Im}_{p}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\}
. In particular, we have
\tilde{e}-1\geq c+k
. Let us define the function
f_{E}:\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{k}\}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that, letting
f_{E}(a_{0}):=c^{\prime}
, for all
1\leq i\leq k
:

f_{E}(a_{i}):=\begin{cases}f_{E}(a_{i-1})&\text{ if }a_{i-1}\equiv a_{i}\mod 2\\
f_{E}(a_{i-1})+1&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Since for all
1\leq i\leq k
, we have
f_{E}(a_{i})\leq f_{E}(a_{i-1})+1
, we have
f_{E}(a_{k})\leq c^{\prime}+k
. We set
e^{\prime}:=f_{E}(a_{k})
. Note also that, for all
1\leq i\leq k
, we have
f(a_{k})\geq c^{\prime}
. With this choice, we have
\mathsf{Sz}(E^{\prime})=e^{\prime}-c^{\prime}\leq k\leq\tilde{e}-1-c=\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)-1=n-1
.

We can now define the function
p^{\prime}
. We let
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
for
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
. Then, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}
, we let:

p^{\prime}(o):=\begin{cases}p(o)\in[0,1]&\text{ if }p(o)\in[0,1]\\
u\in[0,1]&\text{ if }p(o)=\tilde{e}\\
k_{f_{E}(\max(c,n))}\in K_{e^{\prime}}&\text{ if }p(o)=k_{n}\in K_{\tilde{e}-1}\end{cases}

Let us now show a useful property about the function
f_{E}
from Definition
84
.

Lemma 85
.

Consider an (arbitrary) environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
and the function
f_{E}:\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{k}\}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
from Definition
84
. It ensures the following: for all
0\leq i\leq k
,
a_{i}
and
f_{E}(a_{i})
have the same parity. In addition, for all
X\subseteq\{a_{0},\ldots,a_{k}\}
,
\max X
has the same parity as
\max f_{E}(X)
.

Proof.

Let us show by induction on
i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
the following property
\mathcal{P}(i)
:
a_{i}
and
f(a_{i})
have the same parity. The property
\mathcal{P}(0)
straightforwardly holds since
a_{0}=c
and
f_{E}(a_{0})=c^{\prime}
have the same parity. Assume now that
\mathcal{P}(i-1)
holds for some
1\leq i\leq k
. If
a_{i}
has the same parity as
a_{i-1}
, then
f_{E}(a_{i})=f_{E}(a_{i-1})
which has the same parity as
a_{i-1}
. Similarly, if
a_{i}
does not have the same parity as
a_{i-1}
, then
f_{E}(a_{i})=f_{E}(a_{i-1})+1
. Hence,
a_{i}
and
f(a_{i})
have the same parity. Overall, the property
\mathcal{P}(i)
holds for all
i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
. The second part of the lemma comes from the fact that
f_{E}
is non-decreasing.
∎

Let us now show that the value of local parity games with an arbitrary environment
E
is equal to the value of the parity game with the corresponding relevant environment.

Lemma 86
.

Consider an (arbitrary) environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
and the relevant environment
E^{\prime}=\langle c^{\prime},e^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle
from Definition
84
. Let
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
and
Y^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{F},E^{\prime})
. Then,
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})\leq\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
.

To prove this result we will use a result whose proof is not yet published. Specifically, consider a game
\mathcal{G}=\langle\mathcal{C},W\rangle
(with
W\subseteq Q^{\omega}
an arbitrary Borel winning objective), a finite set of labels
\mathsf{Label}
and a labeling function
\mathsf{lb}:Q\rightarrow\mathsf{Label}
. Assume that
W
is
\mathsf{lb}
-uniform, that is, for all
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in Q^{\omega}
, if for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathsf{lb}(\rho_{i})=\mathsf{lb}(\rho^{\prime}_{i})
, then
\rho\in W\Leftrightarrow\rho^{\prime}\in W
. In that case, the value of the game
\mathcal{G}
can be approximated (i.e. the value can approached arbitrarily closely) by label-strategies (for both players), that is strategies that only depend on the labels of the states visited (and the current state). For Player
\mathsf{A}
, such a strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
would satisfy that, for all
q\in Q
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in Q^{n}
such that for all
0\leq i\leq n
, we have
\mathsf{lb}(\rho_{i})=\mathsf{lb}(\rho^{\prime}_{i})
, then
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\rho\cdot q)=\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\rho^{\prime}\cdot q)
. This result follows from an adaptation of Martin’s proof of the determinacy of Blackwell games and should be submitted in the next few months. We will explicitly mention in the next proof when we use this result.

Proof.

We let
Q_{y}
and
q_{Y}\in Q_{Y}
(resp.
Q_{Y^{\prime}}
and
q_{Y^{\prime}}
) denote the set of non-stopping states in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}
and the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}\in Q_{Y}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
and the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}\in Q_{Y^{\prime}}
).

Recall the function
f_{E}
from Definition
84
. We let
f:=f_{E}
and we extend it such that
f(\tilde{e}):=d
with
d
the smallest integer of the same parity than
e
such that
d\geq e^{\prime}+1
. Then, we define an alternate game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}=\langle\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},\mathsf{col}^{f}\rangle
which differs from the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
only in the coloring function and in the objective. The coloring function
\mathsf{col}^{f}
in the arena
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
is such that, for all states
q\in Q_{Y}
, we have:

\mathsf{col}^{f}(q):=f(\max(c,\mathsf{col}(q)))

where
\mathsf{col}
refers to the coloring function in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
. Let us show that the value of both games
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}
and
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
is the same. Consider a pair of strategies
(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}})\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\times\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}=\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}\times\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}
. First, we have:

\sum_{x\in[0,1]}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot x]\cdot x=\sum_{x\in[0,1]}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot x]\cdot x

In addition, since all states
k_{i}
loop back to
q_{Y}
in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}
and
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
, we have:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[Q_{Y}^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot q_{Y})^{\omega}]=0=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[Q_{Y}^{\omega}\setminus(Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot q_{Y})^{\omega}]

Furthermore, consider some infinite path
\rho\in(Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot q_{Y})^{\omega}
visiting infinitely often the central state
q_{Y}
. Let
X_{\rho}:=\{\mathsf{col}[\mathsf{InfOft}(\rho)]\cap\llbracket c,e\rrbracket\}
denote the set of colors, at least
c
, seen infinitely often in
\rho
in
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
. Note that
X_{\rho}\neq\emptyset
since
\mathsf{col}(q_{Y})=c
. We also let
X_{\rho}^{f}:=\{\mathsf{col}^{f}[\mathsf{InfOft}(\rho)]\cap\llbracket c^{\prime},d\rrbracket\}
denote the set of colors, at least
c^{\prime}
, seen infinitely often in
\rho
in
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}
. By definition of
\mathsf{col}^{f}
, we have
X_{\rho}^{f}=f[X_{\rho}]
. Therefore, we have, by Lemma
85
and by definition of
d
:

\displaystyle\max X_{\rho}\text{ is even }\Leftrightarrow\max f(X_{\rho})\text{ is even }\Leftrightarrow\max X_{\rho}^{f}\text{ is even }

It follows that:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[Q_{Y}^{\omega}\cap W_{Y}]=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[(Q_{Y}^{f})^{\omega}\cap W_{Y}^{f}]

As this holds for all pair of strategies
(\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}})\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\times\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}=\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}\times\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}
, it follows that
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{Y})=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}}(q_{Y})
.

Let us now relate the games
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}
and
\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}
. By using the unpublished result mentioned prior to this proof, one can show that almost-optimal Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}
can be chosen such that, for all
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in Q_{Y}^{*}
, if for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathsf{col}^{f}(\rho_{i})=\mathsf{col}^{f}(\rho^{\prime}_{i})
, then for all
q\in Q_{Y}
, we have
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\rho\cdot q)=\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}(\rho^{\prime}\cdot q)
. Such strategies are called
(\llbracket c^{\prime},d\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{f})
-uniform strategies. Similarly, in
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
, almost-optimal strategies (for both players) can be found among
(\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{\prime})
-uniform strategies in
\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, any strategy
(\llbracket c^{\prime},d\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{f})
-uniform strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
can be seen as a (
(\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{\prime})-
)strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, a
(\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{\prime})
-uniform strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
can be seen as a (
(\llbracket c^{\prime},d\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{f})-
)strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
up to defining what it does once the state
k_{\tilde{e}}
— colored by
d
— occurs.

Consider any Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}
that is
(\llbracket c^{\prime},d\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{f})
-uniform in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
. As mentioned above, it can be seen as a Player-
\mathsf{A}
strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
. Let
\delta>0
and consider a
(\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{\prime})
-uniform Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
. Consider the Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}}
that mimics the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
as long as
k_{\tilde{e}}
is not seen. Once it is seen, the Player-
\mathsf{B}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}
switches to a
\delta
-optimal strategy in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}
.
By definition of
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}
, and since
u=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{Y})=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}}(q_{Y})
, we have:

\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})\cap(Q_{Y}^{f})^{*}\cdot k_{\tilde{e}}]\leq\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}[(Q_{Y}^{f})^{*}\cdot k_{\tilde{e}}]\cdot(u+\delta)

Since the outcomes leading to
k_{\tilde{e}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{y}^{f}
lead to the stopping state
u\in[0,1]
in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
, and all outcomes leading to a stopping state in
\mathcal{C}_{y}^{f}
lead to the same stopping state in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
, it follows that:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})\cap Q_{Y}^{*}\cdot(k_{\tilde{e}}\cup[0,1])]\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{\prime})\cap Q_{Y^{\prime}}^{*}\cdot[0,1]]+\delta

In addition, we have:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[(Q_{Y}\setminus\{k_{\tilde{e}}\})^{\omega}]=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[(Q_{Y^{\prime}})^{\omega}]

In addition, consider any outcome
o\in\mathsf{O}
such that
p^{\prime}(o)\notin[0,1]
or equivalently such that
p(o)\notin[0,1]\cup\{k_{\tilde{e}}\}
. Then, we have
p^{\prime}(o)=k_{f(\max(c,n))}
where
n\in\llbracket 0,\tilde{e}-1\rrbracket
is such that
p(o)=k_{n}
. Then, we have
\mathsf{col}^{\prime}\circ p^{\prime}(o)=f(\max(c,n))=\mathsf{col}^{f}\circ p(o)
. Furthermore, we have
\mathsf{col}^{\prime}(q_{Y^{\prime}})=c^{\prime}=\mathsf{col}^{f}(q_{Y})
. It follows that, assuming that
[0,1]\cup\{k_{\tilde{e}}\}
is not reached in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
and that
[0,1]
is not reached in
\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}}
, the colors seen with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}
are the same as the colors seen with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f}
(because the strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}
only depend on the colors seen). Therefore:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})\cap(Q_{Y}\setminus\{k_{\tilde{e}}\})^{\omega}]=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{\prime})\cap(Q_{Y^{\prime}})^{\omega}]

Overall, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q_{Y})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})\cap(Q_{Y})^{*}\cdot(k_{\tilde{e}}\cup[0,1])]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y}^{f},q_{Y}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{f})\cap(Q_{Y}\setminus([0,1]\cup\{k_{\tilde{e}}\}))^{\omega}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{\prime})\cap(Q_{Y^{\prime}})^{*}\cdot[0,1]]+\delta

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{\prime})\cap(Q_{Y^{\prime}})^{\omega}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{C}_{Y^{\prime}},q_{Y^{\prime}}}^{\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}^{\delta}}[W(\mathsf{col}^{\prime})]+\delta

Since this holds for all Player-
\mathsf{B}

(\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket,\mathsf{col}^{\prime})
-uniform strategies, and for all
\delta>0
, it follows that
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q_{Y})\leq\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}}[\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}](q_{Y^{\prime}})+\delta\leq\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}}(q_{Y^{\prime}})+\delta
. Since this holds for all Player-
\mathsf{A}

(\llbracket c,d\rrbracket,\mathsf{col})
-uniform strategies
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{A}}^{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
, it follows that
\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{Y})=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}^{f}}(q_{Y})\leq\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}}(q_{Y^{\prime}})
.
∎

We can now proceed to the proof of Proposition
57
.

Proof.

Consider some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. First, consider the case where
n=0
. Then, an environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle
with
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)=0
is such that
c=e
is even. That is, the corresponding parity game
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}
is, from Player
\mathsf{A}
’s point-of-view, a safety game. Hence, positional optimal strategies exists for Player
\mathsf{A}
in
\mathcal{G}_{(\mathcal{F},E)}

[
9
]
. This is similar for Player
\mathsf{B}
. Furthermore, there is no relevant environment of size at most -1. Hence, the equivalence holds for
n=0
.

Assume now that
n\geq 1
. Consider any relevant environment
E
with
\mathsf{Sz}(E)\leq n-1
. Then, we have
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)\leq n
. Therefore, if
\mathcal{F}
is positionally maximizable w.r.t. Player
\mathsf{A}
up to
n
, then
there is an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy in
\mathcal{F}
for Player
\mathsf{A}
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{F},E)
.

Consider now an (arbitrary) environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
with
\mathsf{Sz}_{\mathsf{A}}(E)=n\geq 1
. Let
\tilde{e}:=\mathsf{Even}(e)
. Clearly, the outcomes in
\mathsf{O}
leading, w.r.t.
p
, to
q_{\mathsf{init}}
of color
c
, can be redirected to
k_{c}
of color
c
(which then loops back to
q_{\mathsf{init}}
) without changing the outcome of the parity game induced by
E
. Hence, without loss of generality, we assume that no outcome
o\in\mathsf{O}
is such that
p(o)=q_{\mathsf{init}}
. We can therefore consider the relevant environment
E^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O})
from Definition
84
of size
\mathsf{Sz}(E^{\prime})=n-1
. Let
Y:=(\mathcal{F},E)
and
Y^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{F},E^{\prime})
and assume that there is a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
that is optimal w.r.t.
Y^{\prime}
. Let us show that
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
is also optimal w.r.t. to
Y
. We let
q_{Y}
denote the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
and
q_{Y^{\prime}}
denote the state
q_{\mathsf{init}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}
. Let
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y}}(q_{Y})
and
u^{\prime}:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y^{\prime}}}(q_{Y^{\prime}})
. By Lemma
86
, we have
u\leq u^{\prime}
. We want to apply Lemma
21
.

Let us show that the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}^{Y}_{\mathsf{A}}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})
ensures item
(i)
of this lemma, i.e. that it dominates the valuation
v_{Y}^{u}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
. This amount to show that
\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y}^{u}\circ p\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\geq u
. Note that, again by Lemma
21
, we have that
\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y^{\prime}}^{u^{\prime}}\circ p^{\prime}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\geq u^{\prime}
. Furthermore, we have:

v_{Y}^{u}\circ p+(u^{\prime}-u)\geq v_{Y^{\prime}}^{u^{\prime}}\circ p^{\prime}

Therefore, for any Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}})
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y}^{u}\circ p\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})+u^{\prime}-u

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y}^{u^{\prime}}\circ p^{\prime}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\sigma_{\mathsf{B}})\geq\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y}^{u^{\prime}}\circ p^{\prime}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\geq u^{\prime}

Since this holds for all Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategies
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{F})
, it follows that

\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathcal{F},v_{Y}^{u}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}})\geq u

Consider now item
(ii)
from Lemma
21
. Consider some Player-
\mathsf{B}
action
b\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}
and assume that
\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[0,1]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)=0
. If we have
\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[k_{\tilde{e}}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0
, then it follows that
\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)\cup\{c\})
is even since
\tilde{e}
is even and is the highest integer appearing the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y}
. Assume now that
\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[k_{\tilde{e}}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)=0
. Then, it follows that, by definition of
p^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{(p^{\prime})^{-1}[0,1]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)=0
. Therefore, by Lemma
21
, it follows that
\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p^{\prime},\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)\cup\{c^{\prime}\})
is even. Furthermore, by definition of
p^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle f_{E}[

\displaystyle\{i\in\llbracket c,\tilde{e}-1\rrbracket\mid\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[k_{i}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0\}\cup\{c\}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{i\in\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket\mid\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{(p^{\prime})^{-1}[k_{i}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0\}\cup\{c^{\prime}\}

We have
\max\{i\in\llbracket c^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rrbracket\mid\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{(p^{\prime})^{-1}[k_{i}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0\}\cup\{c^{\prime}\}=\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p^{\prime},\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)\cup\{c^{\prime}\})
is even. Therefore, by Lemma
85
, we have
\max\{i\in\llbracket c,\tilde{e}-1\rrbracket\mid\mathsf{out}_{\langle\mathcal{F},\mathbbm{1}_{p^{-1}[k_{i}]}\rangle}(\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)>0\}\cup\{c\}=\max(\mathsf{Color}(\mathcal{F},p,\sigma_{\mathsf{A}},b)\cup\{c\})
is also even.

In fact, the Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F})
satisfies item
(ii)
of Lemma
21
. Therefore, it is optimal w.r.t.
Y
.
∎

E.3
Proof of Lemma
61
, Page
61

Proof.

Consider a relevant environment
E=\langle c,e,p\rangle\in\mathsf{Env}(\mathsf{O}_{n})
and
Y_{n}:=(\mathcal{F}_{n},E)
. We let
u:=\chi_{\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}}(q_{\mathsf{init}})
. Note that since
E
is relevant, we have
c\in\{0,1\}
and
p:\mathsf{O}_{n}\rightarrow[0,1]\cup K_{n}
. For all
o\in\mathsf{O}
, if
p(o)\notin[0,1]
, we let
n_{o}\in\llbracket 0,e\rrbracket
denote the integer that
t
is mapped to w.r.t.
p
, i.e. the integer ensuring
p(o)=k_{n_{o}}
. Note that, since
E
is relevant, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}_{n}
, we have
n_{o}\geq c
.

Let us introduce some notations. We let
X_{[0,1]}:=\{o\in\mathsf{O}_{n}\mid p(o)\in[0,1]\}
,
X_{\mathsf{even}}:=\{o\in\mathsf{O}_{n}\mid n_{o}\text{ is even}\}
and
X_{\mathsf{odd}}:=p^{-1}[K_{n}]\setminus X_{\mathsf{even}}
.
We let
X_{\mathsf{Lp}}:=p^{-1}[K_{n}]=X_{\mathsf{even}}\uplus X_{\mathsf{odd}}
. Note that we have
\mathsf{O}_{n}=X_{[0,1]}\uplus X_{\mathsf{Lp}}
. We define the valuation
v:\mathsf{O}_{n}\rightarrow[0,1]
mapping each outcome to its value in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
such that, for all
o\in\mathsf{O}_{n}
:

v(o):=\begin{cases}p(o)&\text{ if }o\in X_{[0,1]}\\
u&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

That is,
v=v_{y_{n}}^{u}\circ p
.
For all
x\in\mathsf{O}_{n}
, we let:

w(x):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }x\in X_{\mathsf{even}}\\
0&\text{ if }x\in X_{\mathsf{odd}}\\
p(x)&\text{ if }x\in X_{[0,1]}\end{cases}

For all
x\in\mathsf{O}_{n}
, the value
w(x)\in[0,1]
corresponds to the value of the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
if
x
is seen (indefinitely, if it is in
X_{\mathsf{Lp}}
). This can be generalized to a pair of outcomes: for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{O}_{n}
, we let:

w(x,x^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}w(x)&\text{ if }x,x^{\prime}\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}\text{ and }n_{x}\leq n_{x^{\prime}}\\
w(x^{\prime})&\text{ if }x,x^{\prime}\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}\text{ and }n_{x^{\prime}}\leq n_{x}\\
p(x)&\text{ if }x\in X_{\mathsf{[0,1]}},x^{\prime}\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}\\
p(x^{\prime})&\text{ if }x^{\prime}\in X_{\mathsf{[0,1]}},x\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}\\
\frac{1}{2}\cdot(p(x)+p(x^{\prime}))&\text{ if }x,x^{\prime}\in X_{\mathsf{[0,1]}}\end{cases}

Figure 18:
The game form
\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

Finally, we let
\mathcal{T}_{n}
denote the
2\times 2
game form at the top left of the game form
\mathcal{F}_{n}
from Figure
9
. It is depicted in Figure
18
. We let
u^{\prime}:=\mathsf{val}_{\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle}
. Now, there are several cases:

•

Assume that
y,z\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}
. Then,
u\in\{0,1\}
, and playing
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{n}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{n}
) is optimal for Player
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
) w.r.t.
Y_{n}
.

•

Assume now that
y\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}
and
z\in X_{[0,1]}
. Then,
u=p(z)
, as argued in the proof sketch, and playing
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{A}}^{n}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}\in\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{B}}^{n}
) is optimal for Player
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
) w.r.t.
Y_{n}
. This is similar if
y\in X_{[0,1]}
and
z\in X_{\mathsf{Lp}}
.

•

Assume that
p(y),p(z)\in[0,1]
and
p(y)\leq p(z)
. In that case, we have
u=\frac{3p(y)+p(z)}{4}
and playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
(resp.
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
) is optimal for Player
\mathsf{A}
(resp.
\mathsf{B}
) w.r.t.
Y_{n}
.

•

Let us now assume that
y,z\in X_{[0,1]}
and
p(z)<p(y)
. In that case, we have:

\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}\leq u\leq\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}

We argue that Player
\mathsf{B}
always has an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy w.r.t.
Y_{n}
.

–

If
u=\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}
, then positionally playing action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
is optimal for Player
\mathsf{B}
. We now assume that
u<\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}
.

–

If, for some even
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i})\leq u
, then playing action
b_{i}
is optimal. We now assume that for all even
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i})>u
(i.e.
x_{i}
is mapped to either a real greater then
u
or even integer). Furthermore, if for any even
i\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})\leq u
, then playing action
b_{i,i-1}
is optimal. We now assume that for all even
i\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})>u
.

–

It follows that, for any odd
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
,
w(x_{i})\leq u
and
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})\leq u
— otherwise Player
\mathsf{A}
could ensure that the value of the game is more than
u
by playing the corresponding row, i.e. action
a_{i}
or
a_{i,i-1}
. It also implies that
u^{\prime}\leq u
. Indeed, assume that it is not the case, i.e.
u^{\prime}>u
. Consider a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathcal{D}(\{a_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{b}}\})
whose value in the game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle
is greater than
u
. Then, the Player-
\mathsf{A}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
that plays the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
in
q_{\mathsf{init}}
parity dominates the valuation
v_{(\mathcal{F}_{n},E)}^{r}
for some
r>u
7
7

7

Potentially, if it is not already the case, up to playing action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
with small yet positive probability.
, due to the assumptions we made in the previous item. In fact,
u^{\prime}\leq u
.

Note that this implies
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}\neq\emptyset
. Indeed, assume that
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}=\emptyset
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Consider a Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
in the game form
\mathcal{T}_{n}
that plays action
a_{\mathsf{t}}
with probability
1-\varepsilon
and action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
with probability
\varepsilon
. Then, for some small enough, yet positive,
\varepsilon>0
and since
\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}>u
and
v(x_{0}),\ldots,v(x_{n-1})=u
, such a
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy has value more than
u
in the game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle
. Hence,
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}=\emptyset
.

–

Let us exhibit a Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy that is optimal w.r.t.
Y_{n}
. Consider a Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\{b_{\mathsf{l}},b_{\mathsf{r}}\})
that is optimal in that game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle
. Let
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
be the Player-
\mathsf{B}
positional strategy in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
that plays the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
at
q_{\mathsf{init}}
. Recall that we assume that for any odd
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
,
w(x_{i})\leq u
and
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})\leq u
,
\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}\leq u
and
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore, the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
dominates the valuations
v_{Y_{n}}^{u}
and no Player-
\mathsf{A}
action can, against the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
, make the game loop indefinitely on
q_{\mathsf{init}}
while ensuring that the highest color seen is even, almost-surely. In fact, this strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
parity dominates the valuations
v_{Y_{n}}^{u}
. Hence, the Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
is optimal w.r.t.
(Y_{n})
, recall Lemma
21
.

Let us now consider the case of Player
\mathsf{A}
.

–

If
u=\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}
, then playing action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
is optimal. We now assume that
\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}<u
.

–

It follows that, for all even
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i})\geq u
and for all even
i\in\llbracket 2,k-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i,i-1})\geq u
. Otherwise Player
\mathsf{B}
could play the action
b_{i}
or
b_{i,i-1}
and ensure that the value of the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
, from
q_{\mathsf{init}}
, is less than
u
.

–

Furthermore, for any odd
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, if either
w(x_{i})\geq u
or
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})\geq u
, then playing action
a_{i}
or action
a_{i,i-1}
is optimal. We now assume that, for all odd
i\in\llbracket A,k-1\rrbracket
,
w(x_{i})<u
and
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})<u
.

–

Assume that
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}\neq\emptyset
. Then, we have
u^{\prime}\geq u
. This is analogous to the previous case for Player
\mathsf{B}
. Indeed, assume that
u^{\prime}<u
. Then, consider a Player-
\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}\in\mathcal{D}(\{b_{\mathsf{l}},b_{\mathsf{r}}\})
whose value in the game in normal form
\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle
is less than
u
. Then, a Player-
\mathsf{B}
positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
that plays the
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{B}}
in
q_{\mathsf{init}}
parity dominates the valuation
v_{Y_{n}}^{r}
for some
r<u
, due to the assumptions we made in the previous item and since we assume that
\frac{3p(z)+p(y)}{4}<u
. In fact,
u^{\prime}\geq u
.

Therefore, in the case where
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}\neq\emptyset
, Player
\mathsf{A}
has an optimal
\mathsf{GF}
-strategy w.r.t.
Y_{n}
that consists in playing optimally (in
\mathcal{D}(a_{\mathsf{t}},a_{\mathsf{b}})
) in the game in normal form
\mathsf{val}{\langle\mathcal{T}_{n},v\rangle}
, due to the assumptions of the previous item.

–

Let us now assume that
X_{\mathsf{Ex}}=\emptyset
. Since, for all even
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i})\geq u
, it follows that
n_{x_{i}}
is even and at least equal to
c
. Similarly, since for all odd
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i})<u
. That is,
m_{x_{i}}
is odd or less than
c
. In addition, for all even
i\in\llbracket 2,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i,i-1})\geq u
. That is,
n_{x_{i}}>n_{x_{i-1}}
. Similarly, for all odd
i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
w(x_{i},x_{i-1})<u
, it follows that
n_{x_{i}}>n(x_{i-1})
. We have
c\leq n_{x_{0}}<n_{x_{1}}<\ldots<n_{x_{n-2}}<n_{x_{n-1}}\leq e
. This cannot happen if
e-c\leq n-2
.

Let us now exhibit a relevant environment
E
of size
n-1
w.r.t. which Player
\mathsf{A}
has no optimal strategy. Consider the environment where
c:=0
,
e:=n-1
,
p(z):=0
,
p(y):=1
and for all
i\in\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket
, we have
p(x_{i}):=k_{i}
. We do have
\mathsf{Sz}(E)=n-1
. Furthermore, the value
u
of the parity game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
from
q_{\mathsf{init}}
is equal to
\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}=\frac{3}{4}
. Indeed, Player
\mathsf{B}
ensures that
u\leq\frac{3}{4}
by playing action
b_{\mathsf{Ex}}
. Furthermore, for all
\varepsilon>0
, Player
\mathsf{A}
can play with probability
1-\varepsilon
action
a_{\mathsf{t}}
and with probability
\varepsilon
action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
, thus ensuring that
u\geq\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon
.

Consider now an arbitrary Player-
\mathsf{A}

\mathsf{GF}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and the corresponding positional strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
in the game
\mathcal{G}_{Y_{n}}
. If
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
plays with positive probability action
a_{\mathsf{b}}
or action
a_{\mathsf{Ex}}
, then the value of
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is less that
u
since
\frac{p(y)+3p(z)}{4}<\frac{p(z)+3p(y)}{4}
(Player
\mathsf{B}
can play the action
b_{\mathsf{r}}
). However, if
\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
does not play these actions with positive probability, then consider the Player
\mathsf{B}
strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
that plays action
b_{\mathsf{l}}
with probability 1. It ensures that the value of the strategy
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
is 0. The reason why is the following: the highest index that the variables
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}
are mapped to w.r.t.
p
is
n-1
and it is odd. Furthermore, all variables
x_{1},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n-1}
are mapped to odd indices. Finally, the highest index that the variables
(x_{1},x_{0})
are mapped to is
1
and it is odd. This is also the case for
(x_{3},x_{2})
, …,
(x_{n-1},x_{n-2})
. Hence, the highest color seen infinitely often with
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathsf{s}_{\mathsf{B}}
is almost-surely odd, and surely no stopping state is reached.
∎
